Benefits from broadening real asset allocations 
The vulnerability of balanced equity/bond portfolios to higher inflation, especially 
stagflation regimes, points to benefits from larger allocations to real assets. ‘Real 
assets’ also provided diversification in Tech-led equity drawdowns. Due to size and 
liquidity constraints we included only Gold in our ‘World Portfolio’ benchmark. Over the 
long run, Gold had a low Sharpe ratio but occasionally, like in recent years or the 1970s 
stagflation, it delivered very strong returns and diversification for balanced equity/bond 
portfolios. But Gold is an incomplete inflation hedge; while it protected purchasing 
power over the long run, it had a mixed relationship with inflation over shorter horizons – 
in recent years it has performed well mainly due to concerns about currency 
debasement and institutional credibility. 
Adding a broader set of real assets can better diversify different sources of 
inflation. If inflation is demand-driven, companies with pricing power and cyclical 
commodities benefit, while cost-push inflation due to energy supply shocks favours 
related commodities. While real asset stocks (infrastructure/real estate/energy) cannot 
provide an imminent inflation hedge they protect from inflation in the medium-term and 
have higher Sharpe ratios. We include the equal-weight index of Gold, S&P GSCI and 
different real asset stocks – as Exhibit 36 shows, compared to Gold alone this ‘Real asset 
index’ received a larger weight in a ‘regime-neutral’ portfolio since 1950. While the 
World Portfolio is currently very exposed to innovation, it might not have enough 
protection from inflation, pointing to higher allocations to real assets.

The impact of FX and the investor currency 
As US weights in global equity and bond benchmarks have risen, the Dollar has become a more important 
driver of risk-adjusted returns for non-US investors (Exhibit 37). The impact of FX risk in portfolios depends 
on foreign asset exposure, home-currency volatility relative to foreign-currency exposure, and correlations 
10 July 2026   21
Goldman Sachs Global Strategy Paper

### Page 22

with equities. The Dollar was a drag for non-US investors in the early 1990s and into the GFC, but has 
generally boosted Sharpe ratios since then, supported by its uptrend and negative correlation with risky 
assets. This was especially true for investors with cyclical currencies such as the Australian Dollar, Swedish 
Krona and Canadian Dollar, while investors with safe-haven currencies such as the Swiss Franc and 
Japanese Yen generally saw lower Sharpe ratios up until the COVID-19 crisis.

Both optimal regional equity allocations and FX hedge ratios vary by investor currency. We split global 
equities into five buckets: domestic equities, US equities hedged to domestic FX, US equities unhedged, 
World ex US equities unhedged and World ex US equities hedged. We then look at average 10-year rolling 
optimal weights (Exhibit 38):  Since 2010, large unhedged US equity weights benefited most investors, 
though less so before the Dollar strengthened. Hedge ratios and allocations also differed by currency: Swiss 
investors, for example, generally required higher FX hedge ratios and larger domestic equity allocations 
because of the Swiss Franc’s strength and safe-haven characteristics. This highlights both the benefits and 
challenges of incorporating FX in long-horizon portfolio construction.

### Page 23

Finding the right balance between Innovation and Inflation

Late cycle investing — time in the market vs. timing the market  
Even though there is a case for leaning against the drift to equities and Technology 
stocks in the medium-term, being underinvested can be costly in the short-term, 
with equities usually performing well up until the bull market peak. Avoiding large 
S&P 500 drawdowns would unsurprisingly boost performance but missing positive 
returns would have been very costly. Thus, timing the market always competes with time 
in the market. Corrections tend to be short-lived and thus difficult to time. And while 
bear markets are deeper and longer, the average profile since 1900 shows that investors 
that divested 6 months before the peak roughly lost as much as those that were 6 
months late (Exhibit 39). It is more important to avoid large losses after the start of a 
bear market.

Leading sectors also tend to continue outperforming into the bull market peak 
(Exhibit 40). As mentioned earlier, this was particularly pronounced during Tech booms 
like in the 1950s and 60s as well as the late 1990s – this makes leaning against sector 
momentum particularly costly. This is particularly true when leading sectors are being 
driven by earnings growth, as is the case today or was the case for Financials leading into 
the GFC. However, during Tech booms, price momentum can continue without 
fundamentals – during the Tech Bubble, Tech stocks continued to perform until 2000 
although profitability for the TMT sector peaked around 1996 (Exhibit 41).

Exhibit 39: Equities tend to perform well into bull market peaks

### Page 24

Five investment ideas to balance innovation and inflation in portfolios 
Investors need to keep exposure to innovation, protect against inflation and 
improve risk mitigation within portfolios. While a growing Tech sector in benchmarks 
and portfolios makes portfolios imbalanced and more risky, leaning against a Tech boom 
is difficult. In most scenarios, long-term equity returns might be below long-run average 
but until momentum for the Tech sector more clearly moderates it is difficult to lean 
against it. We investigate five different risk mitigation strategies to help investors stay 
invested and create better balance in multi-asset portfolios. 
(1) Keeping it real — selectively increasing real asset exposure  
Allocations to real assets help create more balance in multi-asset portfolios in case 
of inflation risks, and they improve the potential for harvesting real returns. 
Historically, during some of the biggest Tech sell-offs, real assets also proved defensive – 
on average our equal-weight ‘Real Asset Index’ delivered positive returns during TMT 
sector drawdowns, particularly during the Tech Bubble burst (Exhibit 42). ‘Real Asset 
stocks’, i.e. an equal-weight index of infrastructure, real estate and energy stocks, 
generally outperformed during those Tech drawdowns and helped diversify equities.

### Page 25

With the energy shock from the Middle East war reversing and a sharp decline in oil 
prices, the benefits from different real assets are changing. During supply shocks 
linked to energy, direct commodity exposure, i.e. GSCI and oil exposures, as well as 
short-dated breakeven inflation are the main hedges, especially when central banks are 
fighting inflation. If central banks are not fighting inflation and there are concerns about 
institutional credibility and currency debasement, Gold can usually provide 
diversification. As our commodities team recently reiterated, there is still a case for 
diversification into commodities - commodities that are more demand-driven such as 
those linked to decarbonisation, EVs and renewable energy, as well as AI infrastructure 
might take over from energy. While commodities fare less well when inflation is 
normalising, real estate and infrastructure tend to perform best and would get further 
support if central banks are shifting less hawkish.

(2) Don’t lose Tech momentum, diversify it through style 
Diversifying across styles within equities can help manage risks around Tech stock 
momentum. During previous TMT sector drawdowns, we found that low volatility, high 
dividend yield stocks outperformed and there were large rotations from growth to value 
(Exhibit 44). An extreme example of the diversification benefits was the Tech Bubble 
burst when low volatility and high dividend yield stocks were up with S&P 500 down 46% 
and the TMT sector down 79%. This is in part due to them benefitting from lower bond 
yields but even during the 2022 drawdown without large decline in bond yields they 
managed to outperform materially. While cyclicals did not underperform much during 
TMT drawdowns, there was some benefit from defensive exposures. 
The ratio of momentum vs. low vol stocks nearing Tech Bubble peaks best illustrates

the risk of a reversal and need for style diversification (Exhibit 45). The recent leg in 
the momentum rally has been led by global Tech hardware stocks, especially 
semiconductors, leading the market higher. As a result, index concentration has 
increased in both the S&P 500 and MSCI Asia ex Japan. This points to potential for more 
dispersion and increasing benefits of diversification within the AI ecosystem. In addition 
with concerns about AI and disruption of capital-light business models there is potential 
for more rotation towards more capital-heavy sectors, which also fits with allocations to 
‘real assets’ – our European equity strategy team has dubbed this the HALO effect: 
Heavy Assets, Low Obsolescence, and we also have similar baskets in the US, Europe, 
Asia, EM and Japan (Exhibit 46).

(3) Don’t get lost in translation - managing US asset dominance 
We expect continued benefits from regional diversification and managing Dollar 
risk. Since last year, there has been less clear US asset outperformance (see also Global 
Strategy Paper: Magnificent Diversification). Since the start of 2025, the S&P 500 has 
lagged major global equity indices, in both local currency and US Dollar terms. 
Historically, during Tech-led equity sell-offs, US equities have on average 
underperformed other markets because they are more closely linked to innovation. FX

performance has been more mixed, but during the Tech Bubble burst the Dollar 
weakened, especially in 2002. The Swiss Franc has proven particularly defensive, 
although recent SNB interventions have changed that ‘risk-off’ characteristic. As our FX 
team recently highlighted, the Dollar and Tech sector performance have become more 
closely linked. A US equity market sell-off with clearer RoW outperformance would likely 
be a catalyst for Dollar depreciation.

In the event of a US recession, JGBs and EM bonds might provide more 
diversification within global multi-asset portfolios. The Yen recently traded at its 
weakest level versus the US Dollar in 40 years, while JGB yields are nearing multi-year 
highs. As our FX team recently showed, the USD/JPY has recently decoupled from real 
rate differentials due to rising JGB term premia (Exhibit 48). However, the Yen often 
outperforms when markets are pricing in a US recession, with Fed cuts and both equity 
and real rates lower. While near-term our markets team expects the weaker Yen/ higher 
JGB yield trend to extend, eventually it might present an attractive hedge for a Tech-led 
US equity drawdown. Similarly, EM assets, which are negatively correlated with the 
Dollar, can diversify US asset-heavy portfolios (Exhibit 49) - our EM team has also found 
that EM assets have become more resilient in ‘risk off’ episodes.

### Page 28

(4) Keeping all your options — long-dated calls, put spread collars, convertibles  
To improve asymmetry or increase upside convexity in a late-cycle boom, we like 
long-dated call options.  As we have shown before, long-dated call options were a very 
effective tail-risk-management strategy during the Tech Bubble. Replacing equities with 
a portfolio of 2-year 25-delta call options and IG credit can better tailor risk/reward 
profiles in late-cycle backdrops. A 4x option position to reach a starting 100-delta 
generally outperforms during strong rallies and bear markets (Exhibit 50). Investors can 
also size the number of options to match the downside tail risk of equities (6.9x) to 
unlock more upside convexity or create a defensive version (3.2x) to mitigate drawdown 
risk during bear markets. Compared with short-dated options, longer-dated calls 
benefit more from sustained equity rallies (particularly when coupled with rising 
volatility) and carry more efficiently thanks to lower theta decay and a less steep 
volatility term structure.

In bull markets, investors can also hedge equity exposures with short-dated put 
spread collars, which can help protect gains buffering smaller corrections. As we 
showed before, bullish sentiment and strong equity returns are often supported by 
favourable macro conditions, that can linger. However, the elevated risk of corrections 
makes put-spreads collars (i.e. selling calls to fund buying put spreads) on equities 
particularly attractive. This year put/call skew dropped close to historical lows across 
equity regions, sectors, and single stocks on the back of more bullish option positioning, 
making collars cheaper (Exhibit 51). Another smaller asset class that has recently begun 
to outperform is convertibles, which combine low duration with long equity calls, which 
can be beneficial in a late-cycle backdrop. Both during the late 1990s and around the 
GFC, convertibles outperformed vs. a 60/40 portfolio (Exhibit 52).

Exhibit 50: Long-dated equity calls can improve asymmetry or increase upside convexity 
Indexed total return of a portfolio of 2-year calls on S&P 500 (rolled after 1 year) + USD 1-3y IG vs. S&P 
500. Orange shade: late-cycle rallies. Blue shade: Bear markets

Source: Goldman Sachs FICC and Equities, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research

### Page 29

(5) Looking for alternatives — private markets, commodity carry, trend following  
Allocations to alternatives can also improve risk-adjusted returns of the World 
Portfolio in a late-cycle boom. Especially when the return potential and diversification 
in traditional assets are more limited, alternatives can play a more important role. 
Exhibit 53 shows several assets and strategies that could enhance the World Portfolio 
over the medium term through either high Sharpe ratios or diversification benefits. For 
example equity/long short funds might be able to benefit from rising stock dispersion 
within the AI ecosystem. Commodity curve carry might also benefit from structurally 
higher roll yields due to commodity concentration. Finally, private markets may provide 
access to more targeted and diversified sector and style exposures than public markets 
benchmarks.

### Page 30

During periods of elevated inflation, CTAs have often delivered stronger returns, 
irrespective of inflation momentum, in contrast to traditional assets (Exhibit 54). In 
part, this is due to inflation ‘trending’ more while growth shocks happened fast 
historically. As a result, investors may underreact initially, and commodities in particular 
can trend for a long time if supported by fundamentals. Higher inflation also reduces the 
ability of central banks to buffer the business cycle and drive sharp reversals in markets. 
In the last cycle, in particular, large dovish pivots by central banks often triggered sharp 
‘risk on’ rotations during growth slowdowns. Conversely, ‘risk off’ moves, especially in 
equities, have been fast with vol of vol trending up post the GFC. Equity long/short 
hedge funds might help diversify concentrated equity benchmarks and benefit from 
rising return dispersion linked to AI winners and losers. Historically hedge funds 
outperformed their beta to the market during late cycle periods and during the Tech 
Bubble (Exhibit 55).

## Document 387: NZ CPI preview, June quarter 2026 (2026-08-02)

Westpac New Zealand Economics
Pain at the pump
• With the war in the Middle East pushing oil
prices sharply, consumer price inflation is set
to rise to a two-year high.
• We estimate that New Zealand consumer
prices rose by 1.5% in the June quarter.
That’s slightly higher than our previous
forecast, consistent with the firmer than
expected increases in prices for volatile items
like holiday accommodation in Stats NZ’s
June update.
• The annual inflation rate is expected to rise to

4.1% (up from 3.1% previously).
• Core inflation has been softening but remains
above the RBNZ’s 2% target midpoint. That’s
despite the downturn in economic growth and
softness in the labour market.
• Our forecast is close to the RBNZ’s updated
forecast for 3.9% annual inflation.

We expect the June quarter inflation report (out on 21
July) will show that New Zealand consumer prices rose
1.5% over the past three months. That would see the

annual inflation rate rising to 4.1% – up from 3.1% in the
year to March and the highest level in two years.
The main driver of June’s spike in consumer prices has
been the sharp rise in fuel prices since the start of the

Recent months have also seen further large increases in
household energy prices (3% of the CPI). Electricity prices
rose 4% over the June quarter and are up 12% over the
past year. Similarly, household gas prices rose 2% over
the quarter, leaving them up 11% over the past year.
Also adding to inflation has been the 0.4% rise in food
prices over the quarter (18% of the CPI).

Oil spill(overs).
With a sharp rise in energy prices, many businesses

have reported large increases in other operating costs,
including transportation and materials. For instance, in
the construction sector, the cost of PVC piping and other
inputs rose sharply in recent months. Consistent with
that, we expect the cost of building new home (which
makes up close to 10% of the CPI) to take a step higher
this quarter after muted gains over the past year.
But while production costs have been rising, soft demand
has been a brake on many businesses’ ability to pass

those higher costs through into output prices. That will
limit the rise in overall consumer prices but does mean
continued pressure on businesses’ margins.
The risk that increased fuel costs spillover into broader
inflation pressures is a significant concern for the RBNZ.
With that in mind, measures of core inflation will be a key
focus. (Note: core inflation measures smooth through the
large quarter-to-quarter swings in individual prices and
instead track the underlying trend in inflation). In terms of

specifics, we expect:
• CPI ex-fuel inflation: +2.8% yr (vs +3.2% previously)
• CPI ex-fuel and food: +2.9% yr (vs +3.0% previously)
• CPI ex-fuel, food and household energy: +2.5% yr (vs
+2.6% previously)
• 30% trimmed mean: +2.4% (vs +2.3% previously)
Although the various measures of core inflation have
been dropping back, we’re reluctant to describe them
as ‘low.’ Despite a sharp slowdown in economic growth
and softness in the labour market, the various measures

of core inflation have lingered above the 2% midpoint of
the RBNZ’s target range for an extended period. And with
the recent oil related lift in inflation pressures, recent
business surveys point to the risk of at least a temporary
broader lift in inflation over the coming months.
Much of the firmness in core inflation has been due to
increases in domestically oriented non-tradables prices.
We expect these prices will rise 0.6% over the quarter.
That would see annual non-tradables inflation softening

to 3.4%, down from 3.5% last quarter but still well above
average levels. Much of that strength has been related
to continued large increases in administered prices, like
council rates and electricity.
Bucking that trend of general firmness in non-tradeable
prices, however, has been notable softness in housing
rents (11% of the CPI). With abundant supply and low
population growth, average housing rents have shown no
growth since late last year, and regions likely Wellington

have seen outright falls.

On the imported front, higher fuel costs are expected
to see tradables prices rising by a huge 2.6% over the
quarter. That would result in annual tradables inflation
rising from to 4.8% from 2.5%yr previously. But while

higher fuel costs have been a significant driver of the rise

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
in imported inflation, the lower New Zealand dollar is also 
playing an important role. Excluding food and fuel costs, 
we estimate that tradables prices were up 3.2% over 
the past year. The average value of the trade-weighted 
exchange rate (TWI) in the June quarter was almost 4% 
lower than a year earlier.
How do our forecasts compare to the 
RBNZ’s assumptions? 
Under the direction of new Governor Anna Breman, the 
RBNZ has made efforts to strengthen it communications. 
That includes the releases of addition information and 
forecasts updates at its interim policy reviews (previously, 
such information was only released when the more 
detailed quarterly Monetary Policy Statements were 
released). This has been very welcome move from the 
RBNZ, especially given the significant and rapid changes 
in the global economic backdrop in recent weeks. 
The RBNZ’s July update show inflation peaking in the 
year to June at 3.9%. The RBNZ didn’t publish a quarterly 
inflation track in July, but we estimate that their annual 
forecast is consistent with a 1.4% quarterly rate in 
consumer prices. 
Our forecast for a 4.1% rise in consumer prices is slightly 
higher than the RBNZ’s expectations. However, given the 
rapid changes in the economic outlook over the past few 
weeks and related uncertainty about the outlook, a result 
in line with our forecast would not be a major surprise to 
the RBNZ. 
However, even if the overall inflation result is close to 
forecasts, the underlying detail will be a key interest. 
The RBNZ will be watching closely for signs that high fuel 
prices are spilling over into other prices, especially with 
global oil prices taking another step higher recently. With 
that in mind, the strength of the various core inflation 
measures will be a key focus for the RBNZ. 
Updates from the June Selected Price report.
Our updated forecast for a 1.5% rise in consumer prices 
in the June quarter is slightly stronger than our previous 
forecast for a 1.4% rise. That reflects updated data from 
Stats NZ’s June Selected Prices report. Of note, June saw 
a larger than expected increases in the volatile holiday 
accommodation category. That more than offset a more 
modest than expected rise in electricity prices. 
In the other big categories, food prices were up 0.6% over 
the month as expected. Housing rental growth remained 
very muted, rising just 0.1% over the month.

## Document 392: Open-source AI 101_ the battle for the future of AI (2026-07-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Open-source AI 101_ the battle for the future of AI (2026-07-20).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-07-20
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Open-source AI 101: the battle 
for the future of AI

1 
Authors 
Adrian Cox 
Research Analyst

July 21, 2026 
Open-source versus proprietary AI models in a nutshell 
AI-related stocks tumbled at the end of last week on a second DeepSeek 
moment, as the latest Chinese open-weight AI model reminded investors that 
cheap, light alternatives are snapping at the heels of the state-of-the-art 
proprietary models. 
In this brief report for generalists, we explain this latest technology format war: 
1. Open-what? A new twist on age-old format wars 
2. What are open AI models? 
3. Why are they disrupting the markets? 
4. Why would a company give its model away? 
5. What comes next? 
The emergence of DeepSeek’s breakthrough R1 model in January last year wiped 
$600bn off the market value of Nvidia in one day on concerns about whether vast 
hyperscaler capital expenditure was justified in the light of a “good enough” 
model developed in China using older chips and trained for a fraction of the cost.

### Page 2

Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
A similar release on Friday revived those concerns, with the Magnificent Seven
(mostly hyperscaler) stocks shedding 1.8 percent and the Philadelphia Stock
Exchange Semiconductor Index turning down 1.6 percent, bringing its loss for the
week to more than 10 percent. Alphabet’s Google, Microsoft, Amazon’s AWS,
Meta and Oracle are set to spend about $700bn building AI capacity this year, up by
about 70 percent from last year.

Even so, while powerful open models raise questions about the business models of
proprietary model makers and semiconductor demand, they may be bullish for AI
adoption, applications and enterprise customisation. Greater competition, more
efficient models and lower prices may supercharge a new phase of growth.
1. Open-what? A new twist on age-old format wars
Open-source software is nothing new.
n A study by Harvard Business School in 2024 found that about 96 percent of

commercial programs include some code created or distributed for free by
public-facing tech forums. Without it, enterprises would pay 3.5 times
more, or roughly $8.8 trillion, to build their software and platforms, it found.
But open AI models are contentious.
n Some critics saying that once released they may be less secure and more
likely to be misused. (We mainly use the term “open models” for readability
and explain the distinction between open-source and open-weight in the

definition section.)
n Some open models have also been accused of being trained through
“distillation”, using the outputs of more capable proprietary models to
teach a new model, which may breach providers’ terms and magnify errors,
biases or safety weaknesses in the original models. Critics also wonder out
loud about whether some open models have been geared more towards
scoring highly on benchmarks – so-called benchmaxxing – than for solving
real-world problems.

Meanwhile, concerns have been rising about whether proprietary models can be
depended on.
n Anthropic’s most powerful Claude models were recently de facto banned
for several weeks on security concerns. Critics also say calls by proprietary
model makers for safety regulation would in fact protect incumbents.
The debate is reminiscent of past format battles.
n Every great technology needs a rival. In the “war of the currents” of the late
1880s, Thomas Edison defended his direct-current (DC) empire by

portraying the alternating-current (AC) system promoted by George
Westinghouse and Nikola Tesla as lethally dangerous—even supporting
demonstrations in which animals were electrocuted with AC.
n And remember VHS versus Betamax video cassette players in the late
1970s and early 1980s? VHS did not win by being the better technology:
JVC licensed it broadly, manufacturers produced cheaper machines,
recording times were longer and a self-reinforcing ecosystem developed

around it. Open-source AI could follow the same path. It does not need to
beat every proprietary model on every benchmark. It needs to be good
enough, inexpensive and ubiquitous.
21 July 2026
Thematic Research

### Page 3

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
However, while the contest between open and proprietary AI is often presented
as a winner-takes-all format war, the most likely outcome is a combination of the
two.
n At the consumer level, it may resemble Apple and Google’s Android, a
controlled premium ecosystem coexisting with a larger, more
customisable ecosystem. And at the industry level, it may resemble the IBM
personal computer, where widespread compatibility commoditised the

foundational technology while shifting significant value into chips,
software, services and applications built around it. Many organisations will
use both.
2. What are open AI models?
There are three distribution models. For users, it boils down to control and – to coin
a phrase – with greater control comes greater responsibility.
Proprietary, or closed, models are generally the plug and play option, accessed
through the developer's application, an application programming interface (API),

or a managed cloud services.
n For a rough analogy, think of Apple’s iOS operating system or Microsoft’s
Windows, where the company controls the underlying technology and the
terms on which others can use it.
n With proprietary AI models, the developer keeps the model weights,
training methods and most details of the training process private. It controls
pricing, updates and safety restrictions, and can alter or withdraw access.
Prominent examples include OpenAI’s flagship GPT models and

Anthropic’s Claude models.
Open-weight models are open-source-lite.
n Their trained parameters – the billions or trillions of numerical values that
encode much of the model's learnt behaviour – are typically released for
users to download, subject to an applicable licence. You can run the model
on your own infrastructure, adapt or fine-tune it, and technically alter or
remove its safeguards.
n But the release may not include the original training data, the complete

training code or enough information for you to reproduce the model from
scratch. Many of the models referred to casually as open-source – including
DeepSeek’s R1 and Meta’s Llama models, as well as many released by
France’s Mistral – are in fact more accurately described as open-weight.
Genuinely open-source AI systems are much rarer.
n Under the Open Source Initiative, they should give users the freedom to
use, study, modify and redistribute the system, supported by access to its

parameters, training and inference code and detailed information about the
training data. The grand-daddy of open-source is Linux, a free operating
system created in 1991 and found in nearly all cloud infrastructure and the
majority of the world’s servers and mobile devices.
21 July 2026
Thematic Research

### Page 4

Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
Consequently, open-source models often offer:
1.  Lower costs, particularly at scale or for specialised tasks. If you run the
model yourself, you can avoid the developer’s per-token API charges and
can select smaller or compressed models requiring fewer calculations. The
downside? Downloadable does not mean free: you still have to pay for
compute, electricity, engineering, monitoring and maintenance. Open
models may also not be as universally capable as the leading proprietary

systems and may lack their polished integration with wider products and
services.
2.  Greater customisation and control.  You can fine-tune a model for your
industry, language or task, rather than relying on a one-size-fits-all service.
The downside? Although open models are cheaper over the long-run, you
need to invest a lot upfront in infrastructure and expertise. You have to take
more responsibility for testing, guardrails, cybersecurity, updates and

reliability—though managed cloud providers can fill some of the gaps.
3.  Local deployment. You can install some models on your own servers, or
even on “edge devices” such as cars and phones, rather than relying on a
connection to the cloud network run by the provider. That makes them
potentially faster (less "latency") and more secure as you don’t need to
share your data. Once you download it, it’s yours, and API access cannot
be suspended without warning. That is key for some regulated

organisations where data can't leave the premises. The downside? Not
every model can run economically on local hardware, and responsibility for
maintaining performance and protecting the system falls on your
shoulders.

and proprietary (solid colour) models are no longer far ahead of Chinese (red)
and open-weight (white with border) models

### Page 5

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
3. Why are they disrupting the markets?
Open models challenge four assumptions about proprietary models embedded
in AI valuations.
First, scarcity. If capable models can be copied, modified and hosted by many
providers, AI models might begin to look like a commoditised software
infrastructure rather than a uniquely monetisable product.
Secondly, pricing power. AI agents and “tokenmaxxing” employees are guzzling
up vast numbers of tokens, the small units of data that AI models process. As pilots

scale up and AI subsidies come to an end, the leading makers of comprehensive
models are starting to charge for each token rather than a flat rate. For cost-
conscious companies, open models that are good enough for most purposes are
looking increasingly attractive.
Thirdly, capital investment. If you can get something like the same performance
out of an open model with lighter training and inference (running) horsepower, can
the projected spending on chips, networking equipment, electricity and data

centres be justified?
Fourth, US technological moat.  Chinese open models weaken the perceived
durability of US supremacy. The Trump administration says it is in “a race to achieve
global dominance in AI” and has driven policies to strengthen domestic production
and adoption and stifle Chinese access to cutting-edge chips and other technology.
Chinese models have overtaken US models this year in terms of the number of
tokens processed on each by the OpenRouter developer platform.

Figure 3: OpenRouter traffic: trillion tokens going through model platform this
year, broken down by country of model

For more, see our recent report “AI IPOs 101: why they’ll change the boom forever”.
21 July 2026
Thematic Research

### Page 6

Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
4. Why would a company give its model away?
Open models may be commercially viable because producers do not need to
monetise the weights themselves. It’s about losing the battle to win the war.
1. Monetising complementary products
n Developers can charge for convenient hosted APIs, premium
subscriptions, faster inference, enterprise support, fine-tuning, security
and guaranteed service levels. The equivalent of in-app purchases for a free

app gives less tech-engaged customers the best of both worlds.
2. Building an ecosystem and standard
n A widely adopted model attracts developers, tools, optimisations, fine-
tuned variants and research. All of that can improve the model, lower the
cost of acquiring new customers, and make its interface an industry
standard.
3. Commoditising competitors' profit pool
n This is a classic case of commoditising the complement : giving away
something for free (or very little) to stop your rival from earning excess

returns, while increasing demand for (and monetising) your own
surrounding infrastructure. So cloud providers that produce or encourage
open models can benefit from selling compute to host open models;
consumer platforms can benefit from better recommendations and
advertising; and application companies can benefit from lower input costs.
4. Boosting reputation, talent and capital
n In a world where you could fit all of the elite AI researchers (about 5,000) into

a mid-sized concert hall, and where companies are racing to attract capital
to fund exponentially increasing compute costs, a leading open model
demonstrates technical competence, attracts researchers and polishes the
case for fundraising or an initial public offering.
5. Increasing geopolitical influence
n Open models can spread a country’s technical standards, software
ecosystem and cultural standards internationally. For China, capable open
models reduce its dependence on US APIs and present it as the provider of

choice to emerging markets. France has championed Mistral’s open
models as a European alternative. The US is not sitting idle either. OpenAI
itself released smaller, open-weight gpt-oss models as a defensive move
last August, and Thinking Machines launched Inkling, its own open model,
last week.
21 July 2026
Thematic Research

### Page 7

Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
5. What comes next?
History shows that formats can coexist where each offers something different.
n The contest between doesn’t have to be an either/or. With so much at stake,
OpenAI’s honeymoon of a near-monopoly on generative AI in the 18
months or so after the launch of ChatGPT in November 2022 was never
going to last.
n This is in line with the oft-quoted Jevons Paradox, where more efficient
steam engines increased, not decreased, demand for steam power – and

hence coal. Similarly, demand for compute will likely continue to surge as
the market bifurcates into a handful of cutting-edge proprietary models on
the one hand and a myriad of smaller open-source models on the other – just
like trains and cars both found their market and continue to live side by side.
No one ever said: “That’s enough intelligence for now”.
But both will exert a positive gravitational force on each other.
n Open models will force proprietary models to keep a lid on prices and

remain flexible; proprietary models will set the benchmark for industrial-
grade quality, safety and fully integrated products. The competitive moat
will increasingly move away from raw weights and towards the balance of
proprietary data, product design, memory, security and efficiency.
The greatest unknown in this uneasy coexistence is the direction of AI regulation.
n A growing backlash against AI in the US and the West significantly raises
the chance of dramatic policy that could significantly affect its trajectory.

While a handful of key US model makers presents a large target for
legislators, they are also able to speak with one voice in a way that is not
possible for fragmented, often foreign, open-source providers.
n For example, Anthropic released an Advanced AI Framework last month
that called for obligations on frontier AI developers to test their models for
catastrophic risks, engage external evaluators and disclose incidents on an
ongoing basis. Their message echoes warnings from scientists and

economists ranging from job losses to extinction.
n But critics say onerous regulations could favour incumbents with deep
enough pockets to cover the costs and would stifle innovation, putting
national security at risk. Former White House AI tsar David Sacks posted
over the weekend on social media that there was “no reason to limit
American models on tasks that Chinese models handle without issue.
We’re only making ourselves less competitive”.
21 July 2026

Thematic Research

Alphabet (OW, covered by J.P. Morgan US Internet Analyst Doug Anmuth)
reported Jun-Q earnings before the Asia market open today. The key implications
for the Asian tech supply chain are as follows:
Google
•Google’s Jun-Q Capex was US$45bn (+26% QoQ, +100% YoY), in line with

market expectations. Server spending comprised 60% of the Capex while the
remaining 40% of spending was for datacenter build-out and networking
equipment. We believe CSPs have been increasing the server spending mix in
total Capex given their recent Capex raise driven by the component cost hikes.
•Google has raised its full-year 2026 Capex to US$195-205bn (~120% YoY
growth at the midpoint) from US$180-190bn, driven by strong customer
demand and an acceleration in capacity delivery. This is the second round of

Capex increase in a row and implies ~50% HoH Capex growth in 2H26.
Looking into next year, Google expects a significant Capex uptick despite the
high base this year due to undersupplied computing power.
•Google continues to see supply constraints and expects an increase in usage of
third-party capacity in 3Q26 to support the strong demand. Of note, Google
Gemini’s DAU has tripled YoY and the token consumption rate increased 38%
QoQ in 2Q26. This bodes well for the pricing dynamics of NeoCloud vendors

as well as server brands, in our view.
•Google’s cloud revenue was up 24%/82% QoQ/YoY in Jun-Q with continued
margin improvement to 35.6% vs. 32.9%/20.7% in 1Q26/2Q25. Cloud
revenue accelerated significantly from 63% YoY growth last quarter and was
10% better than Street estimates, driven by strength across core GCP, AI
solutions, and AI infrastructure. AI cloud revenue significantly outgrew core
product revenue due to strong demand for enterprise AI products.

•Google Cloud’s backlog increased by US$50bn+ QoQ to US$514bn in 2Q26,
driven by strong demand for Google’s Enterprise AI products. The majority of
the order backlog is related to traditional GCP contracts. Management expects
to recognize 50% of the total backlog as revenue over the next 2 years, which
implies a quarterly run rate of Google Cloud revenue to reach US$30bn+ from
the current level of ~US$25bn.
•Google started delivering TPU systems to customer datacenter in 2Q26 but

expects a muted revenue contribution from the business this year. Management
targets a more significant ramp-up exiting this year and expects a majority of
the TPU revenues from the existing agreements with customers to be in the next
year. This implies stronger TPU-related revenue momentum for the supply
chain vendors next year.
•The strong capex plan bodes well for general servers, AI ASIC servers, and the
GPU server food chain. Key potential beneficiaries include Hon Hai (2317 TT,

## Document 414: The price of political fragility in Europe

Source PDF: all report pdf/The price of political fragility in Europe.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-07-23
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

In Summary  
• 
 With elections ahead in France
 , Spain
 , Greece,  
and 
 Italy in 2027
 , 
political risk will be at 
the center of European government bond markets
 . 
 Our high
 -
frequency Political 
Fragility Index (PFI)  
shows that Europe is 
 at a peak level of 
 political fragility
 . 
Our 
PFI,  
is 
built on polling data across four drivers 
 — 
fragmentation, disaffection, polarization and 
governability.  
T
he 
Netherlands and Belgium sit high on f
 ragmentation (votes splintered 
across a rising number of smaller parties), 
 while France tops the list on  
polarization (rising 
voting share for far
 -
left, far
 -
right, Eurosceptic  
and 
 populist parties)
 . 
Germany and the UK 
show the sharpest increases in fragility since 
 2020  
 both suffering from 
 weakening  
governability
 . 
• 
 Sovereign risk premia 
 (defined as 
 asset swap spreads or ASW)  
have never been more 
sensitive to  
political fragility
 , and  
i
nstitutions matter more than the level of fragility.  
The 
effect of political fragility on risk premia is a one
 -
way ratchet: once triggered, it stays in the 
equation even after the trigger fades. This link, latent under QE, is now structural, with 
sensitivity at a record high (+1 std. dev. in the PFI adds +53
 bps to Italy's ASW). The risk 
premium 
 is purely fiscal
 . Note that 
 despite rising Eurosceptic votes, markets have priced 
out redenomination  
 or euro breakup  
 risk.
Institutionally, majoritarian systems (UK and 
France) carry  
more political tail risk than their fundamentals imply for a given rating. This 
argues for a wider hedge around their electoral and budget calendar, while 
 risk premia  
from consensus system 
 issuers 
 (e.g. Netherlands) tend to stay anchored through political 
noise.  
• 
 To date, and s
 ince the end of QE in 2022, political fragility has resulted in an additional 
interest expense of around EUR 98
 b
n 
(
partly paid and partly still to be paid
 )
, 
when we 
sum up 
 Italy, France, Spain, Belgium and the UK. On average, this 
 represents  
an extra  
3% of their annual debt servicing costs  
past and future
 . 
This burden is  
concentrated in 
the UK 
 (EUR 41bn  
 which represents 
 roughly 
 2.8% 
 of 
 additional debt service costs per 
annum  
over the maturity of the debt
 ) 
and Italy  
(
EUR 
 41bn
 ; 
4
.9%
), 
followed by 
 Spain 
 (EUR 
14bn; 4.5%) 
 and France (
 EUR 
 11bn
 ; 
2
.
3
%
). 
In France's case, most of this additional cost 
stems from the dissolution of parliament in 2024
 . 
Safe 
 haven countries 
 like 
 Germany, the 
Netherlands and Austria  
 show no  
 structural 
 fragility 
 premium yet
 . 
 Their 
 shift from a 
negative to a positive 
 ASW was ma
 i
nly driven 
 by 
a repricing of  
the
ir 
fiscal 
 fundamentals
 . 
However, given their underlying fragility  
this could 
 change quickly
 , a 
 structural  
fragility 
premium 
 could add 1
 -
2% 
 to annual interest expenditure
 . 
 Portugal and Greece were 
excluded given their bailout
 -
distorted financing conditions.  
• 
 France's presidential elections (April 18 and May 2, 2027), followed by Italy's general 
election due by 2027,  
are 
 the 
 next big 
 r
endez
 vous  
between political fragility and the 
bond market.  
 Our 
 PFI  
 shows France's sovereign premi
 a 
 are  
 structurally sensitive to 
political fragility (
 +1 std
 . dev. 
 adds 
 +
38bps  
to the ASW
 )
. 
This 
channel 
 may  
keep pushing 
the OAT
 -
Bund spread toward 
 90bp
 s 
 by year
 -
end, with 
 normalization contingent on 
 a 
parliamentary majority 
 in J
une 2027
 . 
In Italy, fragility is the single most sensitive channel 
in our sample  
(see 
 supra
 ) but 
 it is 
 currently dormant
 . O
 ur baseline holds the BTP
 -
Bund 
spread near 80bps, though a 
 Eurosceptic  
turn on either flank, amplified by the proposed 
majoritarian electoral reform, is a fatter tail that could reawaken the redenomination 
premium France's spread no longer carries.

Political fragility is a major yet hidden factor for pricing European government bonds  
With upcoming elections in France (spring 2027) and Italy (latest in December 2027), political risk will 
be at the center of European government 
 b
ond markets.  
 But it is notoriously difficult to measure
 . 
Political risk has 
 played an important role in  
European government bond 
 prices 
since the euro crisis  
of 
2012
, influencing  
 them in two way
 s
: 
 Political risk  
 can 
 amplif
 y 
 or 
 dampen  
 the risk premia implied by 
economic and fiscal fundamentals 
 depending 
 on 
whether 
the 
political environment 
 is 
perceived as fragile  
or stable
 , and it 
can 
also 
 reflect 
the probability of an 
 intentional 
 Euro 
exit
. This so
-
called “redenomination 
risk” 
is closely related to the  
fragmentation  
of 
 Europe’s 
 political systems under the ascent of populist
 , 
euro
-
sceptic 
 movements.  
 But 
 political risk 
 is 
 notoriously difficult to measure
 , especially on 
 a 
 market
 -
relevant 
 frequency
 , and often 
 remains 
 under 
qualitative  
assessment  
prone to 
 personal  
bias
 es
. 
To 
bring 
this hidden factor into the light  
of 
objective 
measurement
 , we 
develop  
a high
 -
frequency index 
 to 
quantify 
the impact of political fragility on 
 European government bond prices
 , 
and ultimately set a 
 fiscal 
 price 
tag  
on 
political fragility in Europe.   
Our  
 Political Fragility Index 
 (PFI) 
 turns a country's politics into a single weekly number. 
 Our base 
assumption is that we can observe the risk 
 inherent in 
political system
 s 
by observ
 ing the 
 dynamics within 
the 
party system. 
 This is 
a 
crystallization of 
 the 
underlying 
 social 
 cleavages  
and 
 can 
 therefore 
serve 
as a 
proxy for the political system as a whole
 , using 
weekly 
 aggregation
 s 
of 
election 
polls
 , which 
are available 
for 
almost all EU countries plus the UK
 . In 
th
is 
analysis
 , 
we will focus on eight countries: 
 France, Germany, 
Italy, Spain, the UK, Belgium, the Netherlands and Austria
 . 
We leave out Portugal and Greece, whose 
bailout and debt
 -
restructuring programs distorted their 
 financing  
dynamic  
and, with them, our results.  
Based on the polls data
 , 
we can 
 further 
separate 
 four 
dime
nsions of political fragility:   
1) 
 F
ragmentation  
 (30%)
: 
 a
rises 
 when 
 vote
s 
 splinter across 
 a rising number  
 of 
 smaller parties  
(
rescaled Laakso effective number of parties)
 .
1 
2) 
 Disaffection  
(
2
0%)
: 
occurs when 
 voters abandon the traditional governing parties
 , 
which usually 
indicates a weakening of traditional institutions  
(
100 − traditional
 -
party share)
 . 
3) 
 Polarization  
 (20%)
: 
as 
 the result of  
a 
 rising 
 voting 
 share for  
 far
-
left, far
 -
right, 
 Eurosceptic  
 and 
populist parties  
(
the average of far
 -
left
-
plus
-
far
-
right, 
Eurosceptic  
and populist vote shares
 )
.
2 
4) 
 Governability  
 (30%)
:  
 is 
 affected  
 when the government itself leans on 
 political forces  
 on the 
extreme 
r
ight and 
 l
eft of the political spectrum  
(100 − the share of 
not extreme 
governing parties).

 in

 in Europe

 10
, level
 s 
still  
differ from country 
 but

are 
converging  
as safe haven erode
 . 
We can

 of

Sovereign risk premia 
 have never been more sensitive to political fragility  
T
he translation of this political fragility to European Government Bond risk premia is anything but 
mechanical.  
Fragility can remain latent for years without leaving a clear imprint on sovereign debt prices. 
On the other hand, risk premia can reprice strongly on news flow even though the underlying political 
fragility remained unchanged. 
 To measure  
political fragility

marginal driver of

European government bonds are pricing out euro  
 break
 -
up risk almost entirely, despite a rise in 
Eurosceptic votes
 . 
The risk premium on 
 Eurozone  
government bonds embeds two distinct risks: a fiscal 
risk tied to economic fundamentals and a "redenomination risk" reflecting the probability of a country 
exiting the euro, whether by political intent or market accident. By decomposing the risk premium i
 nto 
these two components and examining 
 to which extent they  
respond to changes in the PFI Index, we can 
establish whether markets price political fragility primarily as a fiscal risk or as a 
 e
uro 
break
 -
up 
risk. 
The 
result 
is clear. 
 Before  
the ECB 
 launched  
QE in 2015
 , redenomination  
risk was 
 present  
and accounted for 
almost half of the spread sensitivity 
 for 
Italy and Spain
 . 
Today, sensitivity is driven almost entirely by fiscal 
risk. Redenomination risk has 
 totally  
disappeared since QE, suggesting the emergency mechanisms put 
in place by the ECB and the EU are viewed as credible
 ; 
an exit by market accident is no longer seen as 
plausible. An intentional exit, whether through a Eurosceptic populist government or a referendum, 
remains a tail risk. But it is one  
the market no longer appears to price
 . 
The 
Eurosceptic  
risk seems to have 
shifted entirely from a 
 mix of fiscal and  
implied currency risk 
 to a risk of 
 populist 
 fiscal profligacy (non 
respect of 
 domestic 
 and 
 European budget rules
 ) (Figure 
 5
)
.

The relation between political fragility and sovereign risk premia has become structural.  
 Two 
conclusions follow. First, political fragility is not priced into sovereign premia automatically. A threshold 
effect appears to be at work. But once priced, the effect persists. Second, current sensitivity levels are the 
highest observed to date. 
 Italy and the UK offer instructive case studies.  
The Italian risk premium has long 
shown elevated sensitivity to political fragility
 . 
Historically, however, this relationship was episodic, driven 
by news flow and dis
 tinctive 
political events
 . Therefore, we observe s
ensitivity peak
 s 
during the 2012 euro 
crisis and again with the formation of the Salvini
 -
led populist government in mid
 -
2018 (notably, despite 
ongoing QE). Since 2022, the relationship has become structural, marked by a sustained elevated 
correlation between risk premium 
 and political fragility.  
A similar structural 
 change  
is observable in the 
UK. The 2022 Truss moment 
 establis
 hed a durable link between political fragility and UK sovereign risk 
premium
 , 
mark
 ing  
the onset of a synchronous rise. Prior to this, UK risk premi
 um 
were largely insensitive 
to political fragi
 li
t
y, 
 even the Brexit referendum and the protracted EU exit negotiations left little 
discernible mark  
(Figure 
 6
).

Institutional design matters: 
 W
 hy the same fragility costs more in some 
 countries  
The country the market punishes most for fragility is not the most fragile one.  
The Netherlands looks 
more fragile than the UK, yet the market charges it almost 
 no premium 
 while the UK p
 ays 
 political 
premium
 . 
We see one explanation
 : 
I
nvestors take into account the stability 
 and reliability of the country
 ’s 
institutions. 
 Political  
 science separates two virtues: decisiveness (the power to change policy) and 
resoluteness (the power to commit and not reverse it), which trade off against 
 a system’s 
 number of veto 
players.
 3

A 
centralized 
 majoritarian system like the UK or France concentrates power and is decisive but 
not resolute, so one election can 
 especially 
 rewrite the budget. A 
 federal 
 consensus system like the 
Netherlands  
or 
Germany spreads power, so no single result moves fiscal policy far and its commitments 
are more credible.  
Investors 
 may take into account that 
 a 
 shock  
change in 
 Dutch 
 fiscal  
policy 
 is likely to 
dissipat
 e 
in coalition  
and 
 corporatist  
bargaining whereas a British one 
 will hit immediately
 .  
In m
 ajoritarian systems
 , 
investor
 s 
hedge around 
 the 
 electoral calendar as they 
 carry 
 more political  
tail  
ri
sk
. 
Within 
our 
political fragility  
framework we  
score our eight 
 countries 
acc
 ording to the  
democracy 
type, 
 the 
 active 
 cleavage behind its 
 political 
 p
arty system 
 and 
 the 
 type of 
 welfare state 
 acting  
 as 
 a 
cushion against political fragility 
 (Table 
 2
).

For a given rating, majoritarian issuers carry more political tail risk, while consensus
 -
system spreads 
tend to stay anchored through political noise.  
In our estimates
 , 
a rise in fragility adds around +31bps to 
the premium in the majoritarian systems, UK and France. Among the consensus democracies
 , 
the safe 
havens (the Netherlands, Germany and Austria) barely move. 
 The bond market 
 seems 
not 
to 
charge for 
how fragile 
 the political system 
 is 
– 
as measured by the 
 changes in electoral polls via the PFI
 , but 
rather 
how likely this fragility will translate to financial turmoil and/or affect fiscal sustainability. The threshold 
for majoritarian systems seems to be lower especially when associated with deep active cleavages. The 
welfare state does not have a clea
 r cushioning effect.  
Political fragility 
 accounts 
 for 
2
% to 
5
% of 
annual debt service
 , but only for some countries  
Political fragility 
 can increase annual debt ser
 vice by 
 3
.3
% on average, but the effect  
remains 
 still 
unevenly  
 distributed among European countries
 . 
 We 
 show 
 that 
 increases in  
 political fragility 
 can  
increase sovereign risk premia  
 (
asset
 -
swap spread 
 or 
 ASW)  
which 
 translates into higher  
 government  
funding costs
 . 
Since the end of central bank QE in 2022, we estimate that political fragility has added a 
premium on newly issued European sovereign debt, raising past and future interest expenditure for Italy, 
France, Spain, Belgium, and the UK by a combined EUR 
 98bn. 
In relative 
 terms
, 
t
his premium  
represents 
on average  
about 3
 .3
% of 
 these 
 countries' 
 annual 
 debt service
 , 
ranging from 2% to 
 5
% 
(Figure 7).  
The 
burden is concentrated in the UK and Italy, with a cumulated effect of EUR 
 41bn  
and EUR 
 30bn  
(
2.8
% and 
4
.9% of annual debt service, respectively). Spain and France follow, with a cumulated effect of EUR 
 14bn 
and EUR 
 11bn. 
For France, the increase in debt service can be almost entirely attributed to the June 2024 
parliamentary dissolution. 
 For s
 afe 
 haven countries 
 like 
 Germany, the Netherlands 
 or 
Austria  
political 
fragility 
 has not yet become  
a structural driver  
of 
their 
sovereign risk premia. 
 Their shif
 t from a negative 
to a positive ASW was driven mainly by a changed perception of fiscal supply
 , such as the  
 German 
Sondervermögen
 , 
 not by shifting perceptions of political stability. 
 However, given their underlying 
elevated 
 fragility, it cannot be ruled out that this changes ahead of uncertain elections or during a 
government crisis. Should the premium become 
 structurally 
 priced in, it could quickly amount to 1
 -
2% of 
annual interest expenditure.

The 2027 elections: upside risk to political fragility in France and Italy  
We see 
 a 
risk that the OAT
 -

(

and May 2,  
202
7)

 -

 expect

 s 
by year
 -

(from 80bps today)
 . 
Whether this 
widening proves structural or reverts quickly to fair value  
(that we see around 60bps)
 , 
as has typically 
been the case in prior episodes
 ,

presidency, but critically on whether that candidate can also secure a parliamentary majority  
(elections 
in June 2027)
 . 
Without  
a 
majority, France's majoritarian system 
 heavily passes 
 political fragility through 
to the sovereign risk premium
 . 
This 
makes this 
 French election  
differs from prior ones. In the past, market 
risk repricing around French elections was effectively resolved after the two rounds of the presidential 
vote. This time, full resolution may not come until after four rounds
 , 
following the legislative elections as 
well. 
 However, t
 he nature of the French sovereign risk premium remains entirely related to fiscal 
sustainability, the probability of a “Frexit” is not priced  
- 
at least not for now
 .  
We see upside risk to the Italian spread around the next general election, due by 2027, even though 
our baseline keeps the

BTP
 -

on the

【今月のポイント】
関西経済は、持ち直しの動きがみられる4月の生産は3ヵ月ぶりに前月比プラスとなったが、均してみると横ばい圏で推移している5月の実質輸出は2ヵ月ぶりに前月比でプラスとなった。均してみると横ばい圏で推移している日銀短観6月調査によると、関西の業況判断DIは、全産業で＋17と前回調査から2ポイント上昇。非製造業は悪化したが、製造業が改善した。4月の賃金（大阪）は、名目でプラスが続いている。実質でも5ヵ月連続でプラスとなった5月の小売販売額は名目で前年比プラスが続いている。実質ベースも5ヵ月連続でプラスとなった

## Document 509: 中信：A股调整基本结束，8月修复可期

Source PDF: all report pdf/中信：A股调整基本结束，8月修复可期.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-08-02
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

中信证券认为， A 股本轮调整本质是拥挤交易的修正⽽⾮韩国式去杠杆冲击 ——7 ⽉上涨股
票数量近全 A ⼀半、 ETF 逆势净申购逾 1600 亿元，流动性冲击已基本出清。 8 ⽉普遍修复概
率持续提升，⾮ AI 板块负⾯叙事边际好转，宽基 ETF 加速回流奠定⻛格均衡化基础。
摘要：
A 股本轮调整更多是拥挤交易的修正，⽽⾮韩国式的去杠杆冲击，这体现在： 1 ）整体杠杆情况
⽐较安全， 7 ⽉上涨股票数量接近全 A ⼀半，明显超过 6 ⽉； 2 ）相⽐全球历史上典型的去杠杆⾏
情，当前的融资回落幅度并不算⼤； 3 ） ETF 市场呈现持续的资⾦流⼊，科技类 ETF 的流⼊提供
了充⾜流动性⽀持。当然，局部的流动性压⼒依然存在，尤其是部分⾮核⼼ AI 股，这些个股的调
整影响到了科技板块内部的持仓调整，造成核⼼标的定价短暂失效，不过我们认为⽬前该影响
已基本消除。 8 ⽉普遍修复的概率还在提升，但并⾮简单超跌反弹，⾮ AI 板块的负⾯叙事有边际
好转，资⾦环境也⽀持适当修复。配置上，建议增配能化、有⾊、⾮银和创新药，科技内部需

要借反弹更加聚焦持仓。
本轮调整更多是拥挤交易的修正，⽽⾮韩国式的去杠杆冲击
1 ）整体杠杆情况⽐较安全， 7 ⽉上涨股票数量实际上超过 6 ⽉。截⾄ 7 ⽉ 30 ⽇，全市场融资融券
平均担保⽐例为 264.5% ，虽然较 6 ⽉末的 296.4% 有所回落，但 7 ⽉最低仍达到 261.6% ，融资账
⼾整体具有较为充⾜的安全垫。从市场⼴度看， 7 ⽉共有 2546 只 A 股实现⽉度上涨，明显⾼于 6
⽉的 1419 只；上涨股票占⽐由 25.7% 升⾄ 46.0% 。其中，⾮科技板块上涨股票达到 2,291 只，占
⽐为 50.6% ，表明⾏情并⾮单纯收缩，⽽是由前期⾼度集中的科技交易向更多⾮科技⾏业扩散。
当前市场的调整是科技内部去拥挤与市场结构再平衡，⽽⾮⽜市逻辑的系统性逆转，即使以上
证指数⾃本轮阶段⾼点回撤约 10% 计算，横向⽐较全球主要权益市场，也处于相对温和的⽔平
（韩国 kospi-39% ，⽇经 225 -16% ，标普 500 -4% ）。整体⽽⾔，指数仍具备较强的内⽣稳定

性，科技板块调整并不意味着本轮⽜市⾏情已经结束。
2 ）相⽐全球历史上典型的去杠杆⾏情，当前的融资回落幅度并不算⼤。本轮调整过程中，融资
余额从 6 ⽉ 25 ⽇峰值 3.01 万亿元回落⾄ 7 ⽉ 31 ⽇的 2.59 万亿元，累计降幅约 14% ；历史上融资余
额下降最剧烈的事件为 2015 年中，融资余额低点较峰值降幅达到 60% ；全球过往类似的短期剧
烈调整过程中，融资余额回落区间为 -60%~-14% ，平均回落幅度 -32% ，本次 A 股的融资回落幅
度处于最低⽔平。不过， TMT 板块的融资余额较⾼点回落 20.2% ，超过了 2022 年 3~4 ⽉的 13.2%
和 2024 年初的 13.8% ，仅次于 2025 年的 56.1% 和 2026 年初的 25.3% 。
3 ） ETF 市场呈现持续的资⾦流⼊，科技类 ETF 的流⼊也提供了流动性⽀持。 6 ⽉ 25 ⽇⾄ 7 ⽉ 30
⽇，创业板指阶段性回撤 25.8% ，同期科技类 ETF 累计净申购约 1,655 亿元；其中 15 个下跌交易

⽇中有 13 ⽇录得净申购，下跌⽇净申购合计约 1,159 亿元，若仅统计下跌⽇的正申购规模，则抄
底资⾦约为 1,213 亿元，最⼤单⽇净申购达到 236 亿元。持续存在的增量买盘表明，本轮调整并
⾮市场承接能⼒消失，更接近前期拥挤交易⽡解、机构主动降低集中度，以及 ETF 资⾦逆势承接
共同作⽤的结果，与历史上杠杆冲击所导致的买盘消失和流动性枯竭存在明显区别。
⾮核⼼ AI 股的流动性压⼒造成科技股定价的短暂失效，该影响⽬前已基本出清
此轮调整过程中真正具有短期流动性压⼒的个股主要满⾜股价上涨滞后（主升在今年，过去⼏
年涨幅不⼤）、⾮机构重仓、融资占⽐⾼、前期追涨成本⾼等特征。根据我们测算的流动性压⼒
较⼤的 37 只样本泛 AI 科技股： 1 ）这⼀揽⼦股票 26Q2 的平均股价涨幅⾼达 114% ，远⼤于 26Q1
的 49% ，其中不少个股今年才跟上 AI ⾏情，主要的涨幅也集中在今年 4 〜 6 ⽉，结构上看 AI 上游
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

涨价链条个股居多； 2 ）从参与主体来看，这⼀揽⼦股票均不在 26Q2 主动公募的 TOP30 的重仓
股中，主要是⼀些⾮机构重仓个股，游资、私募和产业资⾦介⼊较多，且融资买盘占⽐较⾼；
3 ） 5 〜 6 ⽉追涨资⾦浮亏较⼤，根据我们测算，截⾄ 7 ⽉ 31 ⽇， 5 〜 6 ⽉买盘的加权成本浮亏的平
均⽔平约为 34% 。
这批⼆线甚⾄三线的 AI 标的，在调整初期跌幅明显⼤于核⼼机构持仓科技股，导致核⼼持仓权重
被动上升，机构资⾦在此阶段实际上难以通过 “ 卖边缘、买核⼼ ” 的⾼切低⽅式实现组合回撤控
制，核⼼票反⽽可能受到牵连。只有当边缘品种先⾏完成筹码出清，进⼊核⼼品种补跌阶段
后，科技类资⾦才具备主动调仓的空间，板块整体的流动性压⼒也随之得到释放。这⼀过程完
成后，科技板块内部的分化会重新展开，基本⾯定价的权重上升，从⽽终结前期的下跌负反
馈。从量化跟踪指标看，我们以前⽂所述 37 只流动性压⼒相对较⼤的泛 AI 科技股作为 “ 边缘科技
持仓 ” 组合，以 10 只机构重仓的、以光模块、晶圆⼚和半导体设备品种为代表的标的作为 “ 核⼼科

技持仓 ” 组合，通过两者的超额收益差判断板块调整的底部信号。从这个指标来看，边缘科技持
仓相对核⼼科技持仓的超额收益已从 6 ⽉末的 31% 快速回落⾄ 7 ⽉ 21 ⽇的 -26% ，随后震荡回升⾄
7 ⽉ 31 ⽇的 -14% 附近，该指标⽬前已呈现⻅底迹象，科技板块整体的流动性冲击已基本结束，
后续将开始分化。
8 ⽉普遍修复的概率还在提升，但并⾮简单超跌反弹
1 ）跌的多的初期反弹多，尤其此前遭遇流动性压⼒的个股。我们测算，在以往类似的流动性冲
击导致的剧烈下跌之后，超跌反弹效应在低点后 5~10 个交易⽇集中兑现，⽽ 10 个交易⽇后的市
场反弹结构则逐步与前期下跌结构脱钩。科技反弹后的卖压可能主要来⾃于下跌过程中过早抄
底的资⾦，以及前期过度集中持仓的机构资⾦调仓。以半导体设备 ETF 为典型，主要的净申购都
发⽣在相关指数⾼点向下刚开始调整的数个交易⽇，这部分持仓可能会成为反弹过程中的卖
压。对于主动型产品⽽⾔，不乏⼀些 6 ⽉⼤幅加仓科技的产品，在 1731 只灵活配置型基⾦中，有

237 只产品 6 ⽉相对双创 50 指数的 Beta 提⾼超过 0.05 ，对应规模 2088 亿元；这部分基⾦ 7 ⽉⽉均
Beta 的中位数仍达到 0.44 ，表明其净值在科技调整阶段仍表现出较⾼的科技敞⼝。这些产品可
能因为过度的偏离，在未来科技反弹的过程中调仓以降低组合波动，这也是制约科技反弹强度
的因素。因此，即使 8 ⽉科技板块整体修复，内部表现也可能明显分化，并⾮前期跌幅越⼤的品
种反弹越强。要从根本上改善当前筹码结构，仍需产业端出现⾜以打开板块想象空间的重⼤突
破，以吸引增量资⾦承接存量筹码的兑现压⼒。
2 ）⾮ AI 的负⾯叙事边际在好转，资⾦环境也⽀持修复。美联储 7 ⽉议息会议整体缺乏实质性鹰
派举措，提到⻓端利率上⾏已带来⼀定⾦融条件收紧，进⼀步表明美联储⽆需急于加息。随后
公布的美国⼆季度 GDP 和 6 ⽉ PCE 数据亦低于预期，进⼀步削弱了短期加息叙事。过去⼏个⽉，
强美元与加息预期是压制⾮ AI 板块需求预期、加剧市场 K 型分化的重要宏观因素，这⼀叙事的边

际动摇，有助于⾮ AI 板块出现⼀定修复。本周政治局会议对经济的定调也趋于谨慎， 4 ⽉强调经
济 “ 起步有⼒、主要指标好于预期 ” ，本次则提出 “ ⾼度重视经济运⾏中的困难挑战 ” ，进⼀步强调
及时谋划增量政策和加⼤逆周期调节⼒度。
与上半年市场由较⾼增⻓预期⼀路向下修正不同，下半年市场将从相对谨慎的预期出发，逐步
等待财政⽀出提速、货币⼯具调整及增量政策落地带来的边际变化。预期运⾏⽅向的转变意味
着，下半年市场结构可能不再只是强者恒强，政策托底和需求预期修复有望推动⾏情由单⼀⾼
景⽓向更多板块扩散。此外，下半年的市场资⾦⽣态也有利于⾮ AI 板块的修复。上半年， A 股
ETF 累计净赎回达到 1.63 万亿元；进⼊ 7 ⽉后， ETF 申赎⽅向明显逆转，全⽉累计净申购达到
4784 亿元， 23 个交易⽇中有 19 ⽇实现净流⼊，已回补上半年累计净赎回的约 29% 。对于主动机
构持仓已经较低的⾮ AI 板块⽽⾔，宽基 ETF 回流形成了更稳定、更均衡的被动承接；叠加前期拥
挤交易松动，市场对⾮ AI 板块的定价约束有望缓和，为 8 ⽉⻛格均衡化和普遍修复创造资⾦基

础。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

建议增配能化、有⾊、⾮银和创新药，科技内部借反弹聚焦持仓
短期市场反弹可能更多具有超跌修复特征，前期跌幅较⼤、筹码出清较充分的个股预计表现更
为突出，尤其是此前受到去杠杆和流动性冲击的⾮核⼼ AI 品种。这类反弹主要来⾃流动性改善、
⻛险偏好修复及空头回补，并不意味着原有产业逻辑和估值体系已经重新确⽴。随着市场流动
性和价格发现机制恢复正常， 8 ⽉配置思路应由交易超跌逐步转向均衡化。借反弹优化持仓结
构，重新回归基本⾯、产业地位和中⻓期盈利能⼒的定价逻辑。我们维持 “ 三个收敛 ” 的中期判
断，即： 1 ） AI 产业链内部，上游硬件及涨价品种相对下游平台、云服务及核⼼应⽤环节的超额
收益趋于收敛； 2 ）⾮ AI ⼯业板块相对海外可⽐公司的估值折价有望阶段性修复； 3 ）科技与⾮

科技板块之间的极致分化趋于收敛。在科技板块内部，建议借边缘品种反弹，及时向核⼼资产
（如光通信⻰头、晶圆制造平台以及半导体设备等）调仓。对于⾮科技板块，重点增配能化、
有⾊、⾮银和创新药。
本⽂来源：中信证券研究

## Document 624: 国常会：高质量建设新型电网，加快推进物流网建设，推动医药科技创新

Source PDF: all report pdf/国常会：高质量建设新型电网，加快推进物流网建设，推动医药科技创新.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 2

### Page 1

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
会议指出，要⾼质量建设新型电⽹，加强统筹协调和政策⽀持，狠抓⼯程质量和安全⽣
产，确保电⽹建设顺利推进，更好满⾜⽇益增⻓的⽤电需求，促进绿⾊低碳转型。要牢固
树⽴健康优先导向，深化医药卫⽣体制改⾰，健全公共卫⽣、医疗服务和医疗保障体系，
推动医药科技创新，加快形成有利于健康的⽣产⽣活⽅式。
摘要：
国务院总理李强 7 ⽉ 31 ⽇主持召开国务院常务会议，学习贯彻总书记关于上半年经济形势和做好
下半年经济⼯作的重要讲话精神。会议强调，要把思想和认识统⼀到党中央关于经济形势的科
学判断上来，坚定发展信⼼、增强忧患意识，以更实举措推动经济持续向新向优向好发展，努
⼒实现 “ ⼗五五 ” 良好开局。要切实提升宏观政策实施效能，⽤好⽤⾜各项存量政策，及时谋划出
台务实管⽤的增量政策。要有效扩⼤国内需求，抓住潜⼒⼤、带动强的领域推出⼀批有⼒举

措，加快实施 “ ⼗五五 ” 规划确定的重⼤项⽬，扎实推进 “ 六张⽹ ” 规划建设。要不断增强发展内⽣
动⼒，在统⼀⼤市场建设、优化营商环境等⽅⾯拿出更多实招硬招。要持续⽤⼒防范化解重点
领域⻛险，扎实做好防灾减灾救灾、安全⽣产等⼯作，加强困难群众帮扶，兜牢⺠⽣底线。
会议听取新型电⽹、物流⽹建设情况汇报。会议指出，要⾼质量建设新型电⽹，加强统筹协调
和政策⽀持，狠抓⼯程质量和安全⽣产，确保电⽹建设顺利推进，更好满⾜⽇益增⻓的⽤电需
求，促进绿⾊低碳转型。要加快推进物流⽹建设，增强枢纽节点、⻣⼲通道功能，完善城乡末
端配送体系，推动跨区域跨⽅式物流规则标准衔接，扩⼤物流信息互联互通和共享开放，着⼒
补短板、畅联通、提能级。
会议研究健康优先发展战略实施有关⼯作。会议指出，要牢固树⽴健康优先导向，深化医药卫
⽣体制改⾰，健全公共卫⽣、医疗服务和医疗保障体系，推动医药科技创新，加快形成有利于
健康的⽣产⽣活⽅式。要⼤⼒倡导 “ 每个⼈是⾃⼰健康第⼀责任⼈ ” 理念，普及健康知识，提⾼全
⺠健康素养，凝聚促进健康事业发展合⼒。

会议审议通过《国务院关于修改〈住房公积⾦管理条例〉的决定（草案）》。会议指出，要更好
发挥住房公积⾦功能作⽤，拓宽提取和使⽤范围，扩⼤制度覆盖⾯，提升管理服务效能，更好
满⾜居⺠多样化住房消费需求。
会议审议通过《国务院关于修改和废⽌部分⾏政法规的决定（草案）》，决定对《全国污染源普
查条例》等 12 部⾏政法规的部分条款予以修改，对《中华⼈⺠共和国防治陆源污染物污染损害
海洋环境管理条例》等 3 部⾏政法规予以废⽌。
会议决定核准辽宁庄河⼀期⼯程等四个核电项⽬。会议指出，要按照全球最⾼安全标准建设和
运营核电机组，压实有关单位主体责任，加强全链条全领域安全监管，务必确保核电安全万⽆
⼀失。（完）
来源：新华社

預估 4Q26 起 AM 產品出貨轉趨暢旺

投資評等維持買進，目標價調整至 92 元，潛在上漲空間 26%： 
1. 受客戶調節庫存影響，預估 2Q26 獲利 QoQ 呈現衰退。 
2. 美國關稅不確定性降低，預估 4Q26 起 AM 產品出貨轉趨暢旺。 
3. 預估 2026 年中國 OE 事業，以小幅獲利為營運目標。

預估 2Q26 EPS 1.34 元，QoQ-17.1%  
受客戶調節庫存影響，東陽 2Q26 營收 56.64 億元，YoY-3.9%，QoQ+0.6%，毛
利率部分，受到產品組合改變影響，預估毛利率由 1Q26 的 32.5%，下降至 32.3%，
獲利部分，預估稅後淨利 7.92 億元，YoY+73.5%，QoQ-17.1%，EPS 1.34 元，(原
估值 1.63 元)。

美國關稅不確定性降低，預估 4Q26 起 AM 產品出貨轉趨暢旺 
觀察 AM 事業營運狀況，1H26 受到關稅較高影響，1-6 月 AM 事業營收 86.47 億
元，YoY-14.9%；展望 2H26，由於 5 月底後美國進口台灣汽車零組件關稅，由原
先的 27.5%，調降至 15%，在關稅不確定性降低下，預估客戶將逐漸加大拉貨力道，
3Q26 為產業淡季，預估季營收 QoQ+4.4%，至 4Q26 進入 AM 需求旺季，客戶
回補庫存力道加大，預估季營收 QoQ+11.3%，AM 產品出貨轉趨暢旺。

預估 2026 年中國 OE 事業，以小幅獲利為營運目標 
觀察中國 OE 事業營運狀況，中國 1-6 月汽車銷售 1,501.6 萬台，YoY-4.0%，而日
系車系受到品牌吸引力下滑，且中國自主品牌新能源車崛起影響，日產 1-6 月銷售
YoY-15.0%，本田 1-6 月銷售 YoY-34.7%，受日系汽車銷售不佳影響，東陽 1H26
中國 OE 事業營收 YoY-12.4%；展望 2H26，東陽持續提升對新能源車滲透率，且
逐漸淘汰落後廠區產能，預估 2H26 營收呈現衰退幅度改善，並達成 2026 年小幅
獲利營運目標。

預估 2026 年 EPS 6.17 元，YoY-4.0% 
展望 2026 年，在上半年受到關稅未確定影響下，預估營收 237.96 元，YoY-5.2%，
毛利率部分，受產品組合改變影響，預估毛利率由 2025 年的 33.6%，下降至 33.1%，
獲利部分，預估稅後淨利 36.52 億元，YoY-4.0%，EPS 6.17 元，(原估值 6.98 元)，
預估獲利自 3Q26 起會呈現逐季改善。

富邦不同之處 
我們預估 2026 年 EPS 為 6.17 元，低於 Bloomberg Consensus 的 6.70 元，我們
認為隨著 2Q26 獲利公布，市場將調低後續獲利預估值。

評價與風險 
東陽過去 2 年的 PE 區間為 12-18 倍，在美國關稅不確定性降低下，預估 4Q26 起，
AM 產品出貨轉趨暢旺，將帶動後續業績成長性，預估股價將往區間中緣值移動，
我們以 2026 年的 PE 評價 15.0x 為目標價， 主要評價風險為中東戰爭規模擴大，造
成汽車零組件銷售大幅衰退。

Sector Rating Definition 
Overweight Sector expected to outperform the broader market indexes within the next 6-12 months 
Market Weight  Sector expected to perform in line with the broader market averages within the next 6-12 months

## Document 683: 怎么看待AI大回调，大摩120页研报深度解读

Source PDF: all report pdf/怎么看待AI大回调，大摩120页研报深度解读.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-08-02
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

摩根⼠丹利指出，回调的主要驱动⼒是技术⾯因素，⽽⾮基本⾯逻辑破坏。市场对 Token 需
求和⾼额资本开⽀回报的担忧不成⽴： AI 调⽤ ROI 超 10 倍，且算⼒效率提升将遵循杰⽂斯
悖论，反⽽推⾼总消耗， GPU 代际升级可⼤幅降本。供给端 “ 电⼒瓶颈 ” 虽存，但快速发电
⽅案、存量设施改造等去瓶颈路径可解。
摘要：
6 ⽉下旬以来，全球 AI 板块经历了⼀轮显著回调。
这到底是短暂的技术性回调，还是周期⻅顶的信号，这是⼀个价值千⾦的问题。
Morgan Stanley 在 7 ⽉ 27 ⽇发布了⼀篇⻓达 120 ⻚的深度报告探讨了这个问题，标题为《 Playing
the AI Infrastructure Dip 》。
报告给出了鲜明的判断：回调的主要驱动⼒是技术⾯因素，包括仓位拥挤度消化、保证⾦融资
去杠杆、动量因⼦反转，⽽⾮基本⾯逻辑破坏。
我仔细阅读了这篇报告，收获不⼩，给⼤家解读⼀下主要内容。
（ 1 ）资本市场担忧⼀： Tokenmaxxing 叙事逆转？
市场近期关注的第⼀个负⾯信号是 "Tokenmaxxing" 叙事的逆转。

有不少企业开始对员⼯的 AI Token 使⽤量施加预算上限，由此引发对⼤模型公司收⼊增⻓可持续
性的质疑。
这个担忧是站不住脚的。
⾸先，当前企业员⼯的 Token ⽀出基数极低，且 ROI 极⾼。
⼤摩对⼤量企业级 AI 应⽤场景的研究显⽰，平均单次 AI 调⽤可节省约 55 美元劳动⼒成本，⽽通
过 Agent 协作完成⼀项企业级任务的平均成本仅为 2-5 美元， ROI 超过 10 倍。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

ROI 倍数在 10x 以上的⼯具，其采⽤与否不是预算决策问题，⽽是核⼼竞争⼒问题，那些没有积
极部署 AI 能⼒的企业将⾯临⽇益显著的竞争劣势。
其次， GPU 代际演进将提升数据中⼼利润率，这意味着未来 Token 可以⼤幅降价⽽不损害盈利能
⼒，进⼀步释放需求。
⼤摩 Intelligence Factory 模型测算显⽰，基于 Blackwell 的数据中⼼ Token 销售净利率约 58% 。
当 Rubin 和 Feynman 代际 GPU 部署后，利润率分别升⾄约 80% 和 90% 。
这意味着 Hyperscaler 可在利润率不变的前提下将 Token 定价压低约 75%，成本曲线的下移空间
⾜够⼤，降价和盈利可以兼得。
（ 2 ）资本市场担忧⼆： Kimi 时刻证伪资本⽀出合理性？
Kimi K3 的发布使资本市场重新审视⼀个核⼼假设：如果中国能以更低成本训练出性能接近的前
沿模型，那么美国 Hyperscaler 每年超万亿美元的 AI Capex 是否⾯临回报率下修？

### Page 3

⼤摩认为，中美⼤模型公司对效率的极致追求，并不会削弱算⼒需求。恰恰相反，它强化了 " 需
求远超供给 " 的结构性判断。
19 世纪经济学家 William Stanley Jevons 观察到⽡特改良蒸汽机⼤幅提升了煤炭燃烧效率，但英
国的煤炭总消耗量反⽽飙升，这是因为蒸汽机变得经济可⾏后被部署到了远⽐之前更多的⼯⼚
和矿井。
因此他提出了著名的 Jevons Paradox ：当某种资源的使⽤效率提升时，该资源的总消耗量不降
反升。
Jevons Paradox 同样适⽤于当前的 AI ⾰命浪潮：算⼒效率提升降低了单位 Token 成本，⽽更低的
成本意味着更多场景、更多⽤⼾、更⾼频次的调⽤，最终推⾼总计算消耗。
⼤摩援引了⼀组数据来量化这种供需失衡的严重程度：
Google ⾼管近期表⽰公司可能需要每 6 个⽉将算⼒翻倍，即 5 年内实现 1000 倍增⻓。
然⽽从供给侧来看， NVIDIA 2025-2028 年 AI 芯⽚销售 CAGR 约 140% ，即便按此速度外推 5 年，

累计交付算⼒也不到 Google 单家需求预测的 10% 。
换⾔之，即使是全球最⼤的算⼒供应商以历史最⾼增速全⼒产出，也仅能覆盖单⼀客⼾需求的
⼀个零头。
（ 3 ）资本市场担忧三：供给侧约束是否构成硬上限？
资本市场的第三个担忧在于：即使需求侧确定性充分，但物理世界的约束是否会导致算⼒基础
设施⽆法按需交付？
⼤摩把物理世界的约束统称 3P ： People ， Power ， Politics 。

### Page 4

People: 数据中⼼建设所需的熟练⼯种（电⼯、焊⼯、管道⼯）处于结构性短缺状态。
Power: 电⽹并⽹排队周期在部分地区已延⻓⾄ 5-7 年，成为数据中⼼投运的最⼤单⼀时间瓶颈。
Politics: 数据中⼼的建设正⾯临从地⽅到联邦的多层级政治阻⼒，且⻛向正在发⽣结构性逆转。
过去⼏年，各州竞相提供慷慨的招商政策来吸引数据中⼼落地，但现在趋势逆转了，各州开始
暂停、附加条件或直接撤销数据中⼼税收优惠。
减缓数据中⼼发展速度、保护居⺠电费免受数据中⼼基建成本波及等议题正越来越多地成为州
⻓竞选平台的⼀部分，预计将成为 11 ⽉选举中的重要选⺠议题。
与此同时，联邦层⾯正试图建⽴全国性的 " 数据中⼼关税 " 。
众议院正在审议Ratepayer Protection Act，这是联邦层⾯⾸次尝试对基础设施建设成本进⾏
分摊⽴法，要求各州公⽤事业公司考虑创建 " ⼤负载标准 " ，让数据中⼼为电⽹升级买单。
此前（ 3 ⽉）， Amazon 、 Google 、 Meta 、 Microsoft 、 Oracle 、 xAI 等已签署⽩宫的 Ratepayer

Protection Pledge ，⾃愿承诺保护现有消费者免受数据中⼼基建成本影响。
该法案将把这⼀⾃愿承诺法律化，实质上形成⼀个全国性的数据中⼼电⼒附加费制度。
⼤摩承认这个担忧的合理性，但将其定性为 "speed bumps" ⽽⾮ structural barriers 。
随着电⽹并⽹排队周期在部分地区超过 5 年，且数据中⼼⾯临越来越⼤的 " ⾃⾏供电 " 的压⼒，现
场⾃发电正成为核⼼解决⽅案。
（ 4 ） Time to Power ：被低估的电⼒时间套利
⼤摩对美国数据中⼼的电⼒缺⼝进⾏了定量测算。
结论是 2026-2028 年美国数据中⼼电⼒需求约 68GW ，扣除在建设施（ 15GW ）和已签约电⽹容
量（ 15GW ），潜在缺⼝达 38GW ，且电⽹并⽹排队周期在部分地区已达 5-7 年。

### Page 5

在这⼀背景下，⼤摩认为电⼒接⼊的时间价值（ Time to Power ）是当前市场定价偏差最⼤的⽅
向。
背后的逻辑链路⾮常清晰：数据中⼼的投产受限于电⼒供应  → 电⽹并⽹排队 5-7 年  → 能够在 1-
3 年内提供电⼒的替代⽅案具备显著的时间套利价值。
⼤摩识别的两条核⼼ " 去瓶颈 " 路径：
⽐特币矿场：这些公司已拥有可观的电⽹并⽹容量和物理⽤地，可直接转化为数据中⼼⽤途
10-19GW 。
⽀持快速部署的发电⽅案：相对电⽹并⽹可提供 1-3 年的时间优势，其中天然⽓轮机可贡献
15-20GW 、燃料电池贡献 5-8GW 。
即便将天然⽓轮机、燃料电池、核电⼚直供、⽐特币矿场转换等 "Time-to-Power" ⽅案纳⼊概率
加权计算，中情景下仍有约 1GW 净缺⼝，悲观情景下缺⼝扩⼤⾄ 11GW 。

### Page 6

换句话来说， Hyperscaler 当前最稀缺的不是 Capex ，⽽是有电可⽤的物理空间，即 “Powered
Shell” 。
⼤摩研究认为当前市场没有充分定价这类 Powered Shell Provider 标的。
为量化这种低估，⼤摩以传统可再⽣能源 PPA 作为参照基准进⾏⽐较，详⻅下表：
当前这类 Powered Shell Provider 的 EV/Watt 仅处于 $2-4 区间，包括 TeraWulf 、 Cipher Mining 、
HUT 8 、 Riot Platforms 、 Applied Digital 、 Galaxy Digital 等公司。
基于成熟数据中⼼运营商（如 Equinix 、 Digital Realty ） 20-25x EV/Watt 的参照，⼤摩给予这批
转型公司 15x EV/Watt 的折价⽬标估值。

### Page 7

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

## Document 732: 海外观察：2026年7月美国FOMC会议：基准情形下，美联储年内或将继续按兵不动

Source PDF: all report pdf/海外观察：2026年7月美国FOMC会议：基准情形下，美联储年内或将继续按兵不动.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

总
量
研
究 
宏
观
简
评  
证券研究报告 HTTP://WWW.LONGONE.COM.CN 请务必仔细阅读正文后的所有说明和声明

[Table_Reportdate] 2026年07月30日  
 [Table_NewTitle] 
基准情形下，美联储年内或将继续按兵
不动 
——海外观察：2026年7月美国FOMC会议

[Table_Authors] 证券分析师 
刘思佳 S0630516080002 
liusj@longone.com.cn

[table_main] 
投资要点 
➢ 事件： 当地时间 7月29日，美联储召开 2026年7月FOMC会议，基准利率保持在 3.50%-
3.75%区间不变。 
➢ 核心观点：7月FOMC会议继续按兵不动，符合市场预期，但3名票委投出反对票，美联储
内部的分歧有所加大。 在通胀方面， 美联储主席沃什继续强调实现2%的通胀目标， 且并不
存在软性的通胀目标，仍略偏鹰派。不过同时他也指出 6月会议后，名义以及实际美债利
率的大幅走高， 市场利率的自我调节形成了金融条件的紧缩， 似乎也在为不加息的政策主
张寻找理由。近期市场消减了9月会议加息的概率，但利率曲线趋于陡峭化。除了强调控
通胀以外，沃什上任后并未给出明确的信号。改革框架明确前，鉴于当前的就业及通胀情
况来看，我们认为美联储在年内仍有可能既不加息也不降息。短期关注 8月杰克逊霍尔会
议上沃什的讲话。 
➢ 美联储按兵不动符合市场预期，分歧有所加大 。7月FOMC会议维持利率不变，符合市场
预期，其中三名地方联储票委投出反对票，支持加息。相对于 6月会议的全票通过来看，
分歧有所加大。而在会议声明方面，较6月会议几乎未变。 
➢ 市场“代替”美联储，完成金融条件的紧缩。从沃什在新闻发布会上的表述来看，似乎在
为不加息的政策主张寻找理由。沃什强调，自6月会议美联储减少前瞻指引后，名义以及
实际美债收益率均大幅走高，市场正在学会“play the ball, not the refere” 。而他本人也在
记者会问答中指出不会把今天的决定称之为 “暂停” ， 因为市场价格作出了反应。 虽然他同
时强调了在必要且适当的情况下，将毫不犹豫的采取行动。但风险在于，如果过度依赖于
市场自我的调整来实现金融条件的紧缩， 可能使美联储落后于利率曲线， 同时也会降低美
联储的公信力。 
➢ 强调实现通胀目标，但也提前预告通胀口径存在变化的可能。新闻发布会上，沃什强调，
并不存在软性的通胀目标，2%就是美联储的通胀目标， 重申实现物价稳定的目标， 这一表
现仍偏鹰派。 但对于未来通胀口径的变化给市场先打下 “预防针” ， 首先至少年内还会坚持
以PCE为通胀指标，但明年1月后工作组可能会有补充，其次他所关注的通胀数据比PCE
更为广泛。 
➢ 年内仍有可能不加息也不降息。发布会召开前后，美股先涨后跌，美元指数回落，美债收
益率曲线陡峭化， 黄金冲高回落。 近期市场削减了9月FOMC会议加息的概率， 转而定价更
高的期限风险溢价。在沃什完成框架改革之前，基准情形下，减少前瞻指引，让市场通过
自我的利率调节去完成金融条件的收和紧可能会成为一种方式。 而从双重目标来看， 当前
难以明确找到加息以及降息的理由：美国经济K型分化，但供需双降的情况下劳动力市场
持稳的门槛仍然不高；油价从3月开始中枢抬升，但目前尚未看到在核心通胀中大幅扩散
传导的明显迹象，在通胀预期没有脱锚的情况下，供给侧主导引起的通胀，美联储利率工
具的直接影响相对有限， 沃什也在发布会上强调更多关心的是通胀趋势， 而非依赖单一数
据。 
➢ 风险提示：中东冲突对通胀影响大于预期，美国就业市场受AI影响恶化超预期。

### Page 2

证券研究报告 HTTP://WWW.LONGONE.COM.CN 2/3 请务必仔细阅读正文后的所有说明和声明

## Document 744: 甘肃区域经济专题月度点评报告：上半年经济平稳运行，新动能持续壮大

Source PDF: all report pdf/甘肃区域经济专题月度点评报告：上半年经济平稳运行，新动能持续壮大.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

策略研究报告
请参阅文后免责条款
事件：
 甘肃省统计局公布 2026年上半年甘肃省经济运行情况。
观点：
 经济增速居全国第二梯队。上半年，甘肃省实现地区生产总值 6959.1
亿元，同比增长 4.9%，高于全国上半年 4.7% 的 GDP 增速，在各省
份中增速位居全国第二梯队。一、二季度经济总体平稳运行，底盘稳
固。分产业看，第一产业增加值 450.2 亿元，同比增长 5.9%；第二产
业增加值 2500.9 亿元，增长 5.3%；第三产业增加值 4008.0 亿元，增
长 4.6%。
 高技术产业增势良好。上半年，甘肃省规模以上工业增加值同比增长
6.7%，高于全国 5.4% 的同比增速。各细分行业中多数保持增长， 39
个行业大类中，22 个行业同比增长，占比为 56.4%，其中高技术产业
增加值增长 7.0%，快于全省规上工业增加值的同比增速 0.3 个百分
点。锂离子电池、铜材、稀土磁性材料、天然气产量分别增长 60.8%、
20.9%、18.4% 和 24.6%，呈现较快增长。

 服务业平稳增长。上半年，甘肃省服务业增加值同比增长 4.6%。其中，
居民服务修理和其他服务业、信息传输软件和信息技术服务业、租赁
和商务服务业、水利环境和公共设施管理业增加值分别增长 24.0%、
23.5%、17.3%和 12.0%。上半年，全省铁路客运量同比增长 5.3%，铁
路货运量下降8.3%；公路客运量同比下降1.7%，公路货运量增长1.9%。
 固定资产投资承压下行。上半年，甘肃省固定资产投资同比下降 8.9%，
低于全国固投-5.7%的增速；扣除房地产开发投资，固定资产投资下降
7.2%，低于全国该项-2.7%的增速。分领域看，基础设施投资同比下降
9.2%，其中道路、铁路投资分别增长 11.4%和 32.5%；制造业投资下降
4.9%；房地产开发投资下降 17.5%。分产业看，第一产业投资下降 4.1%；
第二产业投资下降 11.9%，其中工业投资下降 11.9%；第三产业投资下
降 7.0%。民间投资同比下降 8.0%，扣除房地产开发投资，民间投资下
降 8.3%。

 网上零售快速增长。上半年，甘肃省社会消费品零售总额同比增长
1.1%，低于全国社零 1.3%的同比增速。基本生活类商品零售稳定增长，
限额以上单位粮油食品类、中西药品类、饮料类商品零售额分别增长
7.5%、8.3%和 7.5%；部分升级类商品需求持续释放，通讯器材类、化
妆品类商品零售额分别增长 5.8%和 6.0%，新能源汽车零售额增长
6.4%。上半年线上消费表现亮眼，全省网上商品和服务零售额同比增
长 20.0%，其中网上商品零售额增长 32.9%。

上半年经济平稳运行，新动能持续壮大
——甘肃区域经济专题月度点评报告

### Page 2

策略研究报告
请认真阅读文后免责条款
1
 重点产品出口高速增长。上半年，甘肃省进出口总值同比增长 10.1%，
低于全国进出口 16.9% 的同比增速。其中，出口总值增长 14.7%，高
于全国出口总额 13.4%的增速；进口总值同比增长 8.7%，低于全国进
口总额 22.1%的增速。从出口商品看，未锻造金（银）、铜材、基本有
机化学品、“新三样”产品出口分别增长 2.5倍、2.2倍、26.3%和 47.0%。
 居民消费价格温和上涨。上半年，甘肃省 CPI同比上涨 1.0%，与全国
CPI 涨幅持平。分类别看，食品烟酒及在外餐饮价格同比上涨 0.5%，
衣着价格上涨 1.2%，居住价格下降 0.1%，生活用品及服务价格上涨
1.6%，交通通信价格上涨 1.4%，教育文化娱乐价格上涨 0.6%，医疗保
健价格上涨 1.5%，其他用品和服务价格上涨 9.4%。核心 CPI 同比上涨
1.0%，低于全国 1.2%的涨幅。上半年，甘肃省 PPI同比上涨 8.9%，高
于全国 PPI1.5%的涨幅。

 总结：2026 年上半年甘肃省经济运行总体平稳，GDP 累计增速位列全
国上游，三次产业协同发力，产业结构持续优化。规模以上工业增加
值增速 6.7%高于全国水平，高技术产业、新能源新材料产品产量大幅
攀升，工业企业营收利润大幅改善。服务业平稳增长，数字服务、居
民服务业增速领跑，线上消费快速增长，出口呈现较高增速，新三样
出口拉动作用突出。CPI温和上涨，PPI 涨幅扩大。但固定资产投资整
体承压，地产、制造业投资走弱，社零增速偏弱，市场需求仍有不足。
整体来看，全省经济增长总体平稳，向新向优态势明确，后续聚焦城
市更新、“六张网”建设等重点领域，扩大有效投资，提振消费市场，
不断拓展巩固经济向新向优态势，推动经济实现质的有效提升和量的
合理增长。

 风险提示：
(1)经济风险。经济运行总体平稳，但仍会受到很多不确定因素的影响。
(2)行业风险。行业业绩会受到政策变动、供求关系、行业周期等影响，
若业绩不及预期，基于此的投资决策可能出现偏差。
(3)汇率风险。人民币汇率在合理均衡水平上的基本稳定，有利于市场
表现，但人民币汇率受到多种因素影响。
(4)贸易保护主义风险。全球贸易保护主义事件有所减少但时有发生，

若超预期可能对进出口产生影响，导致供应链变动风险。
(5)黑天鹅事件。难以预计的突发事件对经济产生干扰，这些事件可能
包括地缘冲突、金融风险、自然灾害等。

## Document 790: 美日联手护盘日元，美国真实动机：防止日本抛售美债？

Source PDF: all report pdf/美日联手护盘日元，美国真实动机：防止日本抛售美债？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-08-03
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

美⽇罕⻅联⼿⼲预汇市，名为保卫⽇元，实为华盛顿的 “ 美债保卫战 ” 。为防最⼤海外债主⽇
本抛售美债筹资从⽽冲击⻓端收益率，美国被迫下场。双⽅⾼调借 FIMA ⼯具规避抛压，但
这剂短期猛药终难医治⽇元结构性疲软的根本痼疾。
摘要：
美⽇时隔数⼗年再度联⼿⼲预外汇市场，此举表⾯上是为稳定⽇元，背后却折射出华盛顿对美
国国债市场稳定性的深层忧虑。分析⼈⼠指出，美国参与此次协调⼲预的核⼼逻辑，在于防⽌
⽇本被迫⼤规模抛售美债以筹集⼲预资⾦ —— ⽽这恰恰可能对美债⻓端收益率造成不可承受的
冲击。
本次美⽇联合⼲预是⾃ 1998 年以来两国⾸次携⼿买⼊⽇元，也是继 2011 年东⽇本⼤地震后， G7
层⾯时隔逾⼗年再度联合⾏动。⽇元此前已跌⾄近四⼗年低点，上周四⼀度触及 163.73 兑 1 美
元。⼲预消息传出后，⽇元迅速反弹⾄ 157.57 。特朗普表⽰，美国参与⼲预是对⽇本的⽀持，
也是维护全球经济稳定的举措。
市场对此反应敏感。多名分析师警告，若⽇本被迫单边⼲预且须抛售美债融资，美债收益率曲

线⻓端将承受额外压⼒ —— ⽽今年以来，美国 10 年期国债收益率已累计上升近 57 个基点。美⽇
两国财政部⻔对美联储 FIMA 回购⼯具的⾼调强调，被解读为双⽅竭⼒规避 " 强制抛售美债 " 这⼀
最坏情景的重要信号。
防⽌美债遭抛售：华盛顿的核⼼关切
分析⼈⼠认为，促使美国罕⻅出⼿的关键动因，在于⽇本是美国国债最⼤的境外持有国。
若⽇本财务省单边⼊市买⼊⽇元，所需美元资⾦通常来源于抛售外汇储备中的美国国债。⽜津
经济研究院亚洲经济学主管 Louise Loo 表⽰，这 " 可能是 " 美国选择参与的核⼼原因之⼀。 " 这⾥
存在⾃我保护的成分。若⽇本采取潜在激进的财政政策⾏动，引发市场动荡，可能波及美国国
债市场，进⽽令美元失稳。 "
道富银⾏⾼级宏观策略师 Masahiko Loo 进⼀步指出，⽇本财务省宣布计划将 FIMA 回购⼯具⽤于
未来⼲预⾏动，这⼀信号 " 可能⽐⼲预本⾝更具意义 " 。他解释称， FIMA ⼯具允许外国央⾏在不
直接抛售美债的前提下获取美元流动性，此举等于向市场明确传递信号：⽇本筹集⼲预资⾦⽆

需动⽤美债。

⻛险提⽰及免责条款

128 收藏 分享到 :
写评论
" 突出 FIMA 回购⼯具的可⽤性，向市场表明⽇本可以在不出售美国国债的情况下获取美元流动
性 …… 消除了财务省⼲预可能通过出售短期美债⽽对美国融资市场造成压⼒的担忧。 " 他说， " 这
是⼀种在现有⼯具框架内将信号效应发挥到极致的尝试。 "
溢出效应：⽇本债市与全球债券市场的联动⻛险
华盛顿的顾虑不⽌于⽇元本⾝，还延伸⾄更⼴泛的债券市场联动⻛险。
Masahiko Loo 指出，⽇元持续疲软可能引发⽇本国债（ JGB ）的进⼀步抛售，推⾼⽇债收益
率，并在当前美⽇两国均⾯临⻓期借贷成本上升压⼒的背景下，向全球债券市场蔓延。
Louise Loo 亦补充称，若华盛顿认为⽇本的财政政策正在推⾼⽇债收益率并压低⽇元，协调⼲预
可以为⽇本央⾏赢得时间，以待其在今年晚些时候恢复加息。她强调，⽇元的根本性⾛强需要

依靠更紧缩的货币政策，⽽⾮反复的市场⼲预。
瑞穗证券亚洲（除⽇本外）宏观研究主管 Vishnu Varathan 表⽰，美国的参与使此次⼲预效⼒ " 成
倍放⼤ " 。美国财政部与美联储的介⼊，令市场有更充分的理由相信当局已准备好必要时再度⾏
动。两国政府同时警告 " 绝不迟疑 " 再度出⼿，进⼀步强化了对投机性做空⽇元⾏为的威慑。
质疑之声：⼲预效⼒存疑，技术操作引发困惑
尽管各⽅对此次⼲预的政策意图给予了积极解读，分析⼈⼠对其实际效⼒仍存保留。
令市场困惑的是，有报道称美国此次出售的是欧元⽽⾮美元来购买⽇元，偏离了协调⼲预通常
以美元资产融资的惯例。布鲁⾦斯学会⾼级研究员 Robin Brooks 对此提出强烈质疑，称此举 " 令
市场感到困惑，最终将适得其反 " 。他表⽰： " 这表⾯上可能给⼈留下⼲预⽐以往更有⼒的印象，
但美国参与此事引发的问题多于答案，尤其是美国出售欧元买⼊⽇元这⼀⾮常奇怪的操作。 "
在更深层的结构性问题上， Brooks 明确指出，⼲预⽆法逆转由⽇本债券市场驱动的⽇元贬值趋

势。他认为，⽇本央⾏虽于 2024 年 3 ⽉正式终⽌收益率曲线控制，但仍持续⼤量购买⽇本国债，
这实际上使借贷成本维持在⾃由市场本应厘定的⽔平之下，在此背景下⽇元仍⾯临下⾏压⼒。
Masahiko Loo 也坦承⼲预的局限性： " ⼲预或许能影响未来数⽉的⾛势，但⽇本央⾏的货币政策
正常化进程与对冲资⾦流向，才是决定未来数年⾛势的真正变量。 " 分析⼈⼠警告，除⾮⽇本着
⼿解决驱动⽇元疲软的结构性因素，否则此次协调⾏动未必⽐历次⼲预更为持久。
限免

中興電(1513)  
-  產能增加，帶動獲利持續成長  
投資建議 
標題  
中興電投資評等建議為買進，目標價193元；隱含漲幅21.8%，預估
2026年稅後EPS為9.19元，投資建議在本益比15-21倍區間操作，主要
理由如下： 
(1) 公司在台電、民間儲能等電力設備的訂單滿檔，氣體絕緣開關
（GIS）在手訂單量達400億元以上，長線可看到2031年。 
(2) 公司獲國內晶圓大廠認證的 GIS 設備，預計於第四季正式拉貨，此
專案毛利率較高，帶動整體毛利率增加，並在2027年第一季進入
密集出貨期。

內容摘要 
   中興電預期下半年綠能相關的營收貢獻會有較大的成長   
  受惠台電拉貨放緩的遞延效應已經消除，下半年進入傳統交貨旺季，
且獲國內晶圓大廠認證的 GIS 設備，將於第四季起正式拉貨，在2027
年第一季進入密集出貨期。   
   中興電訂單能見度最遠可看到2031年   
  公司對未來幾年的營運保持樂觀，台電強韌電網計畫持續推進且拉貨
遞延狀況已緩解，加上AI資料中心用電爆發，訂單能見度最遠可看到
2031年。 加上台電在2026年新增的334億元「配電系統五年升級計
畫」也將在2027年擴大拉貨力道。 
   中興電轉投資的嘟嘟房停車可提供穩定的現金流入   
  嘟嘟房停車每年營收約10%以上的成長，可提供穩定的現金流入， 
目前約有5-5.5萬個車位，擁有21萬名以上的Active駕車會員，將於
2027年掛牌上櫃。

### Page 2

:: 訪談內容::

中興電為國內重電設備與電力工程領導大廠 
 1. 公司創立於 1956年，為國內重電設備與電力工程領導大廠，主要
生產用於電力系統保護、供應與計量等用途之各式產品如氣體絕緣
開關設備(GIS)，國內市占率超過85%。 
2. 公司也 提供各式統包變電所、風力水力工程及停車管理服務 (嘟嘟
房)等多角化服務，並於2008年投入氫能燃料電池研發。 
3. 公司憑藉氫能關鍵技術，目前已成功推出「移動車載」和「發電」
兩大應用系列產品，隨著全球燃料電池需求市場逐步顯現，可帶動
相關產品的營收貢獻。 
4. 公司的營收比重分為三大類：綠能、服務和工程及其他。綠能核心
產品為 GIS 氣體絕緣開關（ 345KV、161KV及69KV高壓設備），
涵蓋台電強韌電網計畫、太陽光電標案、微電網、儲能與氫能系
統。 
5. 公司服務類業務以「嘟嘟房」停車場連鎖管理、充電樁營運及後續
維運管理為主、穩定度高，提供不間斷的現金流；工程與其他包含
都市更新、精密加工、消防水電系統整合等 EPC 統包工程。

中興電預期下半年綠能相關的營收貢獻會有較大的成長 
 1. 公司上半 年 綠 能 營 收 比 重 約 占64% 、服務占 21% 、 工 程 與 其 他 占
15%，受惠於訂單結構優化與產能提升，預期下半年綠能相關的營收
貢獻會有較大的成長。 
2. 受惠台電拉貨放緩的遞延效應已經消除，下半年進入傳統交貨旺季，
且獲國內晶圓大廠認證的 GIS 設備，將於第四季起正式拉貨，在2027
年第一季進入密集出貨期。 
3. 公司透過智慧化工廠改造，緩解目前高達  110% 的稼動率，讓下半年
高壓重電訂單的消化速度加快。且針對 AI 資料中心開發的機電與空調
備援系統、模組化機房，預計下半年合作成效將陸續顯現，成為重電
之外的成長動能。

### Page 3

中興電訂單能見度最遠可看到2031年 
 1. 由於算力即電力、電力即國力，政府對電力建設資本支出是無 法停
止的，公司對未來幾年的營運保持樂觀，台電強韌電網計畫持續推
進且拉貨遞延狀況已緩解，加上 AI資料中心用電爆發，訂單能見度
最遠可看到2031年。 
2. 公司在ESG的評等表現上，有助於取得風力發電的工程案。除了太
陽能和風力發電外，水力發電工程也是未來的成長動力。 
3. 焊接是影響公司產出的重要因素，目前有 45個焊接台，人力焊接的
工作小時約 6-8小時/天，台電對焊接的品質很要求，所以公司透過
自動化焊接與  AI報價系統來增加產出效率，預期可提升產能 15%-
20%。 
公司國內各地的廠房與生產設備整理 
廠區 產品 面積(坪) 
南科廠 精密加工 7000 
華亞廠 氫能 4000 
觀音廠 重電 2000 
嘉義廠 重電 11000 
林口廠 重電 -- 
4. 除台電5,600億元強韌電網計畫持續出貨外，台電在 2026年新增的
334億元「配電系統五年升級計畫」也將在2027年擴大拉貨力道。 
5. 公司海外營收占比目前約 5%-10%，重點佈局北美 (隨半導體廠赴
美)、日本、越南及印度市場；氫能事業與太陽能電廠持續推進；停
車場事業與其他能源業務整併效益逐漸顯現，提供多元獲利來源。

中興電轉投資的嘟嘟房停車可提供穩定的現金流入 
 1. 在嘟嘟房停車管理部分，  2023年營收28億元，2024年為33億，預
估2025年可達36億、2026年維持成長的軌道，將於 2027年掛牌上
櫃，目前約有5-5.5萬個車位，擁有21萬名以上的Active駕車會員。 
2. 公司憑藉 特高壓重電工程施工（ GIS 設備與智慧電網）的既有優
勢，在充電樁市場採取「精準質優」的圈地模式，全台充電樁數量
約 500 -600 支（持續增建中） 
3. 高速公路超充防線（國道快充龍頭）第一階段拿下湖口、清水、東
山、西螺等 4 個核心國道服務區服務、共 24 個充電車位 的 8 年營
運權；第二階段獲國道蘇澳、泰安（南北向）、關西、仁德（南北
向）等 6 站共 44 個電動車快充車位 營運權。 
4. 公司配合大樓管委會無法裝樁的痛點，嘟嘟房利用市區  340 多座停
車場作為基地，布設  7KW~11KW 慢充樁與商業快充，達到「停車
即充電」的綜效。

## Document 867: 高盛旗帜鲜明：这是人类历史上最大规模的资本需求周期，美联储只是看客

Source PDF: all report pdf/高盛旗帜鲜明：这是人类历史上最大规模的资本需求周期，美联储只是看客.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-08-03
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

⾼盛认为，全球正从储蓄过剩时代切换⾄资本极度稀缺时代， AI 基建、再⼯业化、国防重
整与主权债务需求同时爆发， 30 年期美债收益率已突破⾦融危机前⽔平，且这⼀趋势与美
联储政策关系不⼤。美联储 “ 更多是乘客⽽⾮司机 ” ，短期内不应期待收益率回落。资本成本
结构性抬升将重写投资范式， 8 ⽉市场将相对平静，但中期前景并不简单。
摘要：
当 AI 、再⼯业化、国防重整与主权债务同时争夺资本，⾼盛认为这场资本争夺战将重写投资范式
—— ⽽美联储，只是这场⼤戏的旁观者。
过去数⼗年，全球经济的底⾊是 " 储蓄过剩 "—— 资本充裕、利率低廉，钱找不到去处。
这个时代正在结束。
⾼盛欧洲对冲基⾦业务负责⼈ Mark Wilson 在其最新周报中直⾔：" 我们正处于⼀个有史以来最
具资本饥渴性的投资周期之中。 "
驱动⼒不是单⼀的。 AI 基础设施建设本⾝已极度消耗资本，但这还只是其中⼀条线。与此同时，
再⼯业化、国防重新投资、电⼒系统重建、供应链在去全球化压⼒下的重新布局，叠加主权国
家为覆盖快速攀升的利息⽀出和不断膨胀的福利开⽀⽽产⽣的融资需求 —— 多条需求曲线同时

向上，资本的价格⾃然被推⾼。
Wilson 的结论是：这种资本竞争 " 很可能是⼀个持久的中期特征，将推动资本定价和成本持续⾛
⾼，从⽽改变相对于近代历史的投资范式 " 。
美联储是乘客，不是司机
本周美国 30 年期国债收益率决定性地突破，升⾄⾦融危机前（ pre-GFC ）从未⻅过的⽔平。
这⼀突破的背景，是新任美联储主席沃什上任后刻意减少前瞻指引，市场对政策路径的不确定
性⼤幅上升。 Wilson 援引历史数据指出： " 回顾 1970 年以来的 6 位美联储主席，伯南克和耶伦在
任职第⼀年各经历了 10% 的回撤，其余 4 位则在第⼀年遭遇了 20% ⾄ 36% 的回撤。 " 新主席上任初
期，市场历来不好过。
但 Wilson 明确表态： " 我认同美联储在这场讨论中更多是乘客⽽⾮司机。 "
换⾔之，收益率上⾏的根本驱动⼒不是货币政策，⽽是前述的结构性资本需求。 " 鉴于开篇所描
述的资本竞争，不要期待这次突破会很快逆转。 "
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

指数平静，⽔⾯之下暗流汹涌
对于只盯着指数涨跌的投资者， 7 ⽉似乎⻛平浪静。但 Wilson 指出， " 指数之下，⾛势是历史性
的 " 。
两条主线同时演绎：
第⼀，个股⾼度分散。 仅看上周五：亚⻢逊单⽇涨 15% ，苹果单⽇跌 10%—— 这是全球市值最
⼤的两家公司，同⽇出现如此分化，本⾝就极为罕⻅。
第⼆，动量因⼦崩塌。 该市场中性组合出现 40% 的跌幅，超越了 2000 年 3 ⽉科技泡沫破裂时的
极端因⼦轮动记录， " 对许多⼈的有效⻛险管理造成了巨⼤困难 " 。
结果是⼤规模去⻛险化。⾼盛 Prime 数据显⽰，基本⾯管理⼈总敞⼝降⾄⼀年低点，净多头回落
⾄底部四分位。 Wilson 认为，经历这番清洗后，市场结构已 " ⼲净得多 " 。
8 ⽉：为何不宜轻易抄底
去⻛险化完成，是否意味着可以反⼿做多？ Wilson 给出了⼏条理由，说明为何 7 ⽉的价格表现不
会就此逆转：
利率突破的影响尚未被市场充分消化，相关再校准仍在进⾏中；

### Page 3

7 ⽉的极端波动（韩国综合指数单⽇涨 18% ， SK 海⼒⼠单⽇涨 26% ）已改变⻛险模型的输⼊
参数，许多机构⽆法快速重新加仓；
视野拉⻓⾄ 3 ⾄ 6 个⽉，前景并不简单 —— 夏后焦点将转向美国中期选举。 Wilson 援引数据：
⾃ 1974 年以来的 13 个中期选举年，标普 500 从 8 ⽉初⾄选举⽇的中位数回报为 0% 。
8 ⽉，⼤概率是⼀个消化期。
基本⾯：盈利强劲，但内部分化
尽管市场波动剧烈，基本⾯的图景却出⼈意料地稳健。
Wilson 指出，与典型年份不同， 2026 年和 2027 年的 EPS 预期在全年内被持续上调。⼆季度业绩
整体亮眼，但⼆阶导数开始分化：美国单季 EPS 增速预计在本季度⻅顶，约为 26% ；⽽欧洲上
半年 EPS 增速为 13% ，下半年有望加速⾄ 19% ，形成罕⻅的后半年强势格局。
存储芯⽚板块则是例外。尽管是年初⾄今涨幅最⼤的板块之⼀，但边际消息⾯已出现恶化信
号：现货 DRAM 价格趋稳，低内存消耗模型技术进展加速，更关键的是 —— 中国存储芯⽚企业

CXMT 完成 IPO 后股价较发⾏价上涨 6 倍，市值突破 5500 亿美元，预⽰未来供给将⼤幅扩张。
超⼤规模云计算企业：资本开⽀ “ 令⼈瞠⽬ ” ，但回报同样惊⼈
本轮财报季最重要的验证命题，是超⼤规模云计算企业能否在上调资本开⽀的同时，给出⾜够
强的收⼊增⻓和 ROI 信号。
答案是肯定的 —— ⾄少对亚⻢逊和微软⽽⾔。
⾼盛⽬前对 Alphabet 、亚⻢逊、微软的资本开⽀预测如下：
Alphabet ： 2027 年 3500 亿美元， 2028 年 4150 亿美元
亚⻢逊： 2027 年 3250 亿美元， 2028 年 3660 亿美元

### Page 4

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

微软： 2027 年 2620 亿美元， 2028 年 2840 亿美元
Wilson 直⾔，这⼀规模 " 令⼈瞠⽬ " 。
但与此同时，业务表现同样震撼：⾕歌云增速加速⾄同⽐ 82% ；微软⾃信地描述企业客⼾正
从 " 前沿模型 " 迁移⾄ " 前沿⽣态系统 " （即在最合适的模型能⼒之间进⾏请求路由的基础设施）；
AWS 收⼊增速加速⾄新冠疫情以来最⾼⽔平。
最具冲击⼒的，是亚⻢逊管理层在财报电话会上的直接表态：
私营部⻔与国家部⻔：⼀个⽭盾的过渡期
Wilson 以⼀个宏观框架收尾：当前是⼀个过渡期。
私营部⻔的超⼤规模企业正在竞相投资，朝着 AI 赋能的未来全速前进；⽽国家部⻔则⽇益受到资
本约束，两者之间的⽭盾将愈发突出。
" 全球经济现实，在某个时刻将迫使⼈们直⾯资本重新定价所必然带来的政治选择 —— 但那是另

⼀天的讨论。 "
眼下， AI 超级周期仍在前⽅， 8 ⽉⼤概率是⼀个相对平静的消化窗⼝。
"AI 收⼊年化规模环⽐⼤幅攀升，⽬前已超过 250 亿美元，同⽐增速达三位数。 "
" 我们看到 AI 业务的利润率和回报率，⽐我们当初建⽴云业务时的轨迹略微领先。 "
" 尽管 2026 年资本开⽀达到 2200 亿美元，我们在 2026 年仍然没有⾜够的产能来满⾜所有需求，
2027 年很可能同样如此，⽽ 2028 年的需求规模已经令⼈震惊 …… 我们⻓期以来认为 AWS 可以成
为数千亿美元的收⼊业务，现在我们相信它⾄少会是这个数字的两倍，很可能成为年收⼊ 1 万亿
美元的业务，并伴随极具吸引⼒的⾃由现⾦流和投资资本回报率。 "
